import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F


def reduce_loss(loss, reduction):
    """Reduce loss as specified.

    Args:
        loss (Tensor): Elementwise loss tensor.
        reduction (str): Options are "none", "mean" and "sum".

    Return:
        Tensor: Reduced loss tensor.
    """
    reduction_enum = F._Reduction.get_enum(reduction)
    if reduction_enum == 0:
        return loss
    elif reduction_enum == 1:
        return loss.mean()
    elif reduction_enum == 2:
        return loss.sum()


def weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight=None, reduction='mean', avg_factor=None):
    """Apply element-wise weight and reduce loss.

    Args:
        loss (Tensor): Element-wise loss.
        weight (Tensor): Element-wise weights.
        reduction (str): Same as built-in losses of PyTorch.
        avg_factor (float): Average factor when computing the mean of losses.

    Returns:
        Tensor: Processed loss values.
    """
    if weight is not None:
        loss = loss * weight
    if avg_factor is None:
        loss = reduce_loss(loss, reduction)
    elif reduction == 'mean':
        loss = loss.sum() / avg_factor
    elif reduction != 'none':
        raise ValueError('avg_factor can not be used with reduction="sum"')
    return loss


def weighted_loss(loss_func):
    """Create a weighted version of a given loss function.

    To use this decorator, the loss function must have the signature like
    ``loss_func(pred, target, **kwargs)``. The function only needs to compute
    element-wise loss without any reduction. This decorator will add weight
    and reduction arguments to the function. The decorated function will have
    the signature like ``loss_func(pred, target, weight=None, reduction='mean',
    avg_factor=None, **kwargs)``.

    :Example:

    >>> import torch
    >>> @weighted_loss
    >>> def l1_loss(pred, target):
    >>>     return (pred - target).abs()

    >>> pred = torch.Tensor([0, 2, 3])
    >>> target = torch.Tensor([1, 1, 1])
    >>> weight = torch.Tensor([1, 0, 1])

    >>> l1_loss(pred, target)
    tensor(1.3333)
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, weight)
    tensor(1.)
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, reduction='none')
    tensor([1., 1., 2.])
    >>> l1_loss(pred, target, weight, avg_factor=2)
    tensor(1.5000)
    """

    @functools.wraps(loss_func)
    def wrapper(pred, target, weight=None, reduction='mean', avg_factor=
        None, **kwargs):
        loss = loss_func(pred, target, **kwargs)
        loss = weight_reduce_loss(loss, weight, reduction, avg_factor)
        return loss
    return wrapper


@weighted_loss
def mse_loss(pred, target, reduction='mean'):
    """Warpper of mse loss."""
    return F.mse_loss(pred, target, reduction)


class MSELoss(nn.Module):
    """MSELoss.

    Args:
        reduction (str, optional): The method that reduces the loss to a
            scalar. Options are "none", "mean" and "sum".
        loss_weight (float, optional): The weight of the loss. Defaults to 1.0
    """

    def __init__(self, reduction='mean', loss_weight=1.0):
        super().__init__()
        self.reduction = reduction
        self.loss_weight = loss_weight

    def forward(self, pred, target, weight=None):
        """Forward function of loss.

        Args:
            pred (torch.Tensor): The prediction.
            target (torch.Tensor): The learning target of the prediction.
            weight (torch.Tensor, optional): Weight of the loss for each
                prediction. Defaults to None.
            avg_factor (int, optional): Average factor that is used to average
                the loss. Defaults to None.

        Returns:
            torch.Tensor: The calculated loss
        """
        loss = self.loss_weight * mse_loss(pred, target, reduction=self.
            reduction)
        return loss


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]

Compute numerator:

a³ =8, b²c = ( (1/√2)^2 )*(1/√2 ) = (1/2)*(1/√2 ) = 1/(2√2). So numerator is (8 + 1/(2√2))^3 ≈ (8 + 0.3535)^3 ≈8.3535^3 ≈ approx 583.

Denominator: a^5 =32, b^4c = (1/√2)^4*(1/√2 ) = (1/4)*(1/√2 ) = 1/(4√2 )≈0.177. So denominator ≈32.177. So the first term is approx 583 /32.177 ≈18.1.

Second term: (b^3 + c²a)^3 / (b^5 + c^4a )

Since b = c =1/√2, so:

Numerator: ( (1/√2)^3 + ( (1/√2)^2 )*2 )^3

Compute:

( (1/(2√2)) + ( (1/2)*2 ) )^3 = ( (1/(2√2) +1 ) )^3 ≈ (1 + 0.3535)^3 ≈1.3535^3 ≈2.468

Denominator: ( (1/√2)^5 + ( (1/√2)^4 )*2 )

Compute:

(1/( (2)^{2.5} ) + (1/4)*2 ) = (1/(5.656) ) + 0.5 ≈0.1768 +0.5 =0.6768

So the second term is approx 2.468 /0.6768 ≈3.648

Third term is same as second term since b and c are equal, so third term is same as second term, so 3.648

So total S ≈18.1 +3.648 +3.648 ≈25.4, which is larger than 12. Hmm, so when a is increased and others decreased, S becomes larger. Maybe the minimal is indeed at a=b=c?

Wait, let me try another case where variables are unequal but not too extreme. Let me set a=4, b=1, so c=1/(4*1)=0.25.

Compute S:

First term: (a^3 + b²c)^3 / (a^5 + b^4c )

a=4, b=1, c=0.25

Numerator: (64 + (1)^2*(0.25))^3 = (64 +0.25)^3 =64.25^3≈ 265,000? Wait, 64.25 cubed is 64.25*64.25*64.25. Wait 64^3 is 262,144, so 64.25^3 would be a bit more. The denominator is (4^5 +1^4*0.25)= 1024 +0.25=1024.25

So first term is roughly (64.25)^3 /1024.25 ≈ (265,000)/1024≈ ~258.8. That's way bigger. So that's worse. So maybe making a larger makes S worse. 

Alternatively, maybe making a smaller. Let me try a=1/2, then b and c must satisfy (1/2)*b*c=1 => b*c=2. Let me set b=2, c=1. So a=0.5, b=2, c=1.

Compute S:

First term: (a³ +b²c)^3 / (a^5 +b^4c )

a=0.5, so a³=0.125, b²c=4*1=4. So numerator: (0.125 +4)^3 =4.125^3≈70.2

Denominator: a^5 +b^4c = (0.5)^5 +16*1=0.03125 +16≈16.03125. So first term≈70.2 /16.03≈4.38.

Second term: (b³ +c²a)^3 / (b^5 +c^4a )

b=2, c=1, a=0.5.

Numerator: (8 + (1)^2*(0.5))^3=(8+0.5)^3=8.5^3≈614.125

Denominator: 32 + (1)^4*(0.5)=32.5. So second term≈614.125 /32.5≈18.9.

Third term: (c³ +a²b)^3 / (c^5 +a^4b )

c=1, a=0.5, b=2.

Numerator: (1 + (0.25)*2)^3 = (1 +0.5)^3=3.375

Denominator: 1 + (0.5)^4*2 =1 +0.0625*2=1.125

Third term≈3.375 /1.125=3.

Total S≈4.38 +18.9 +3≈26.28, which is again higher than 12. Hmm, so maybe when variables are equal, we get the minimal value?

Alternatively, let me test another case where two variables are equal but different from the third. Let's set a = b, so then c =1/(a²). Let me set a =b = t, so c =1/t². Let's compute S in terms of t and see if it can be less than 12.

Let me set a = b = t, so c =1/t².

Compute each term:

First term: [(a³ + b²c)^3]/[a^5 +b^4c] = [(t³ + t²*(1/t²))^3]/[t^5 + t^4*(1/t²)]

Simplify numerator: t³ +1, so numerator is (t³ +1)^3.

Denominator: t^5 + t^4*(1/t²) = t^5 + t².

Thus first term is (t³ +1)^3 / (t^5 + t² )

Second term: [(b³ + c²a)^3]/[b^5 + c^4a] = [(t³ + (1/t²)^2 * t)^3]/[t^5 + (1/t²)^4 * t]

Compute numerator: t³ + (1/t^4)*t = t³ + 1/t³. So numerator is (t³ + 1/t³)^3.

Denominator: t^5 + (1/t^8)*t = t^5 + 1/t^7.

Hmm, so second term is [ (t³ +1/t³)^3 ] / ( t^5 +1/t^7 )

Third term: [(c³ +a²b)^3]/[c^5 +a^4b] = [ ( (1/t²)^3 + t² * t )^3 ] / [ (1/t²)^5 + t^4 * t ]

Simplify numerator: (1/t^6 + t^3) = t^3 +1/t^6. So numerator (t^3 +1/t^6)^3.

Denominator: 1/t^10 + t^5 = t^5 +1/t^10.

Thus third term is [ (t^3 +1/t^6)^3 ] / ( t^5 +1/t^10 )

Hmm, so now S is the sum of these three terms. Let me see if when t=1, which gives the symmetric case, each term is (1+1)^3/(1+1)=8/2=4, so total 12. 

Let me try t=2. Then compute each term.

First term:

(t³ +1)^3 / (t^5 + t²) = (8 +1)^3/(32 +4)=729/36=20.25

Second term:

(t³ +1/t³)^3 / (t^5 +1/t^7). Let's compute numerator and denominator.

t=2: numerator is (<#sep#> gives the direction. So when we compute theta via the angle between vector1 and vector2, the sign is determined by the direction of rotation.

Wait actually, 

The angle theta is the angle between v1 and v2, but when you compute it using the formula:

The numerator is the cross of the vectors (v2.y * v1.x - v2.x*v1.y). The denominator is the dot product.

The angle is calculated using the atan2(numerator, denominator). This gives the angle between them in the correct direction (resulting in a rotation that brings v1 to v2). 

Thus, there is only one rotation, the one equivalent to that direction. 

Wait, that is correct because the angle given by atan2 gives the proper angle for either rotating v1 to align with v2. Because the cross product's sign indicates the direction of rotation.

Thus, there is only one possible rotation matrix for that pair. 

The error occurs therefore when we choose a pair (a,b) from map1 and (x,y) from map2 that do not correspond, but their vectors' lengths are similar (so they pass step 4), but their angle isn't correct. 

But the Rotation computed will not be correct, so many inliers won't be found unless they are part of an even larger correct transformation.

Therefore proceeding with this step.

Once the rotation R is computed and translation T, then this transiton is a candidate model. Now count how many points in map1 when transformed via R and T, map to points in map2 within some threshold distance (like 0.1 units). 

The problem is, doing this for every point in map1 is expensive ( N1 of them, which could be 50k).

Hence, in order to reduce computation, we can choose a subset of sampled points to compute inliers. For example, randomly sample 1000 points from map1, and count inliers among them. 

Even if it's approximate, this would allow for a faster check. Because in RANSAC the core idea is that the best model will have a majority of the data, so the inlier count among a subset would be enough to identify which trial is the best.

Thus the approach:

Use a subset, say S (like 1024 points from map1 + their partners from all of map2).

Wait, alternative scheme:

- After choosing a randomly sampled model (rotation R and T), compute how many points from the entire map1 would have a corresponding point in map2 within threshold:

- Iterate through all points in map1, compute their transformed coordinates, and check if they are close to any point in map2.

To optimize this with a grid structure:

Preprocess map2 into a grid (as a spatial hierarchy) Such as a KD tree or grid cells.

Now, the steps would be:

Preprocessing:

- Process map2 into a grid:

- Choose a cell size of delta (like 0.1), as our threshold. The cell coordinates are rounded (x/delta, y/delta).

- For each point (px, py) in map2, we store it in a dictionary where the key is ( int(px/delta), int(py/delta) ), and the value is a list of (px, py) points in that cell or nearby cells?

Wait it's better to manage for each possible cell (f or floor(x/delta)), (floor(y/delta)), store all points in that cell.

Then, when checking the transformed point (qx, qy):

- Determine its cell (qx_cell, qy_cell) = (floor(qx/delta), floor(qy/delta))

- Then for this cell and adjacent cells (scan around areas sight distance sqrt(2)*delta), but the threshold is sqrt( dx^2 + dy^2) < threshold. Hence checking the same cell and the 8 surrounding cells.

Wait, that would mean for neighboring cells of an entire ring around it?

Alternatively, since delta is the threshold cell size, for a point to be within threshold, it must be in the same grid cell or in one of its eight neighboring cells.

So for a delta of 0.1, and threshold 0.1, any point within the search distance must be in the cells within 0.1 units.

So the nearby cells are:

for cell_x in [qx_cell-1, qx_cell, qx_cell+1]

same for cell_y.

Thus, 3x3=9 cells.

Thus for a transformed point (qx,qy), to check in these nine cells for any map2 points within distance threshold.

If any, then the point is an inlier.

The steps for checking inliers would be for each transformed coordinate (qx,qy):

1. Compute cell indices:

ci_x = floor( qx / delta )

ci_y = floor( qy / delta )

2. For the 3x3 grid of cells around this cell, compute all map2 points in those cells.

3. For each such point p in those cells, compute distance between (qx, qy) and p.

If any is within threshold, the point (qx,qy) has a matching map2 point, so it's an inlier.

The total computation for n1 map1 points would then be for each candidate model:

steps:

precompute:

- map2's grid.

Processing a candidate model (R, T):

count =0 

for each point (x,y) in map1:

compute rotated point (rx, ry) = R^{-1} ? 

Wait wait wait:

Wait, no. The transformation maps map1 point p1 → R*(p1) + T = p2_transformed. 

Wait no: the R transformation is part of rotation R such that when you apply it to a map1 point and translate, it should land on the corresponding map2 point. 

Thus, the transformed point for map1's (x,y) is:

(q_x, q_y ) = R*(x, y) + T 

Wait, earlier computation assumes that the rotation is of the point then adding T.

Wait, yes, according to the analysis earlier, the transformation is: 

R multiplied by (x, y) vector, then adding T vector gives the new position. 

Thus:

The correct transformed coordinates (for each point in map1):

q_x = R00 * x + R01 * y + T.x 

q_y = R10 * x + R11 * y + T.y 

where R is represented as:

    [ R00 R01 ]

    [ R10 R11 ]

So, given R's rotation matrix (based on theta), compute this.

Once (q_x, q_y) are known.

Now, to determine whether there exists a map2 point within threshold:

check the grid cells around q_x and q_y.

Thus, per point in map1:

the steps are O(1) for cell lookup (but with delta chosen as a power of two, for easy coordinate calculation?) 

Thus for N1 points, this would be O(N1 * (number of points in neighboring cells)). However with appropriate delta and map sizes.

Assuming delta is small enough (like 0.1), then per cell has very few points (unless many are in the same place). So this should be manageable.

The total inlier count can thus be computed efficiently. 

Once this is implemented, the RANSAC method can use this, and for each trial's candidate transformation, compute the number of inliers quickly by using the grid.

Thus the overall plan becomes:

Prepare data structures and do the following:

Preprocessing:

1. Read in both map1 and map2 points into arrays.

2. Read the coordinates and store them as a list of tuples for each map.

3. Precompute the grid for map2:

   - Choose delta as the threshold, say 0.1 units (since the coordinates have two decimal places, errors up to 0.005 could exist, but the actual under transformation as well:

   For example, the problem's note indicates the Death Stars were generated with coordinates between -10000 and 10000, so maybe the rotation and translation may have more error but probably the threshold is about 0.3 to cover any errors in rotation and precision.

But perhaps better to set the threshold as 2 * 0.05 to cover the 2 decimal precision (as each coordinate is a float with two decimal digits, which stores it only to the hundredth of a unit. So errors from the given coordinates are +- 0.005 hence the pairwise squared distance between two points might have error up to sqrt(2*(0.005)^2) ) 

But given there are rotations, which introduce more errors. Let’s pick a threshold of 0.5 units (anything within that is considered a match). 

The delta for the grid is set to threshold/1 so that any cells of this size will allow us to check all cells near the transformed point.

Thus delta = 0.5.

So the grid uses 0.5 as cell size. Since the matches must be within some distance.

Thus, for the preprocessing:

   182	        kwargs["v_model"] = kwargs.pop("v_model", True<#sep#>import torch
import numpy as np
import torch.nn as nn
from torch.autograd import *


class ScaledDotProductAttention(nn.Module):
    """
    Scaled dot-product attention
    """

    def __init__(self, d_model, d_k, d_v, h):
        """
        :param d_model: Output dimensionality of the model
        :param d_k: Dimensionality of queries and keys
        :param d_v: Dimensionality of values
        :param h: Number of heads
        """
        super(ScaledDotProductAttention, self).__init__()
        self.fc_q = nn.Linear(d_model, h * d_k)
        self.fc_k = nn.Linear(d_model, h * d_k)
        self.fc_v = nn.Linear(d_model, h * d_v)
        self.fc_o = nn.Linear(h * d_v, d_model)
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.d_k = d_k
        self.d_v = d_v
        self.h = h
        self.init_weights()

    def init_weights(self):
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.fc_q.weight)
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.fc_k.weight)
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.fc_v.weight)
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.fc_o.weight)
        nn.init.constant_(self.fc_q.bias, 0)
        nn.init.constant_(self.fc_k.bias, 0)
        nn.init.constant_(self.fc_v.bias, 0)
        nn.init.constant_(self.fc_o.bias, 0)

    def forward(self, queries, keys, values, attention_mask=None,
        attention_weights=None):
        """
        Computes
        :param queries: Queries (b_s, nq, d_model)
        :param keys: Keys (b_s, nk, d_model)
        :param values: Values (b_s, nv, d_model)
        :param attention_mask: Mask over attention values (b_s, h, nq, nk). True indicates masking.
        :param attention_weights: Multiplicative weights for attention values (b_s, h, n_k, d_v)
        :return: out (b_s, nq, d_model)
        """
        b_s, nq = queries.shape[:2]
        nk = keys.shape[1]
        q = self.fc_q(queries).view(b_s, nq, self.h, self.d_k).permute(0, 2,
            1, 3)
        k = self.fc_k(keys).view(b_s, nk, self.h, self.d_k).permute(0, 2, 3, 1)
        v = self.fc_v(values).view(b_s, nk, self.h, self.d_v).permute(0, 2,
            1, 3)
        att = torch.matmul(q, k) / np.sqrt(self.d_k)
        if attention_weights is not None:
            att = att * attention_weights
        if attention_mask is not None:
            att = att.masked_fill(attention_mask, -np.inf)
        att = torch.softmax(att, -1)
        out = torch.matmul(att, v).permute(0, 2, 1, 3).contiguous().view(b_s,
            nq, self.h * self.d_v)
        out = self.fc_o(out)
        return out


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4])
        ]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'d_model': 4, 'd_k': 4, 'd_v': 4, 'h': 4}]

But given there are rotations, which introduce more errors. Let’s pick a threshold of 0.5 units (anything within that is considered a match). 

The delta for the grid is set to threshold/1 so that any cells of this size will allow us to check all cells near the transformed point.

Thus delta = 0.5.

So the grid uses 0.5 as cell size. Since the matches must be within some distance.

Thus, for the preprocessing:

- map2_grid is a dictionary where the keys are:

cell_x = int( (px)//0.5 )

cell_y = int( (py)//0.5 ) 

Wait precise way:

-> 

for each point (px, py) in map2:

   cell_x = int( math.floor( px / delta ) )

   cell_y = int( math.floor( py / delta ) )

   add this point (or its index) to map2_grid's key (cell_x, cell_y).

The grid is built this way.

Once the grid is built, for each transformed point (q_x, q_y), we determine their cell:

cellq_x = floor( qx / delta )

cellq_y = floor( qy / delta )

then, for each of the cells in cellq_x -1, 0, +1 and same for cellq_y:

for dx in -1,0, 1:

   for dy in -1,0, 1:

      compute cellx = cellq_x +dx 

      celly = cellq_y + dy 

      if the (cellx, celly) exist in the grid:

          get the list of points in that cell.

          for each point p in those cells:

              compute the distance between (qx, qy) and p's coordinates. 

             if < threshold (0.5), then mark hit and break (break after finding first hit to save time?).

Thus for each point in map1, for each transformed coordinate, look in up to 9 cells.

But with the grid setup, each of these cells may contain few points.

Note that if the points in map2 are evenly spread, the number of points per cell is manageable.

Thus, this approach may be feasible.

Thus, with this, the algorithm would be:

Implement Ransac as:

Initialize the current best inlier count and the best transformation (R, T).

max_iterations = 1000 (or higher, like 5000).

threshold_distance = 0.5 

delta_cell_size = threshold_distance 

The grid for map2 is built beforehand.

For each iteration in 1 to max_iterations:

   Randomly select 2 distinct points from map1 : a and b.

   Randomly select 2 distinct points from map2 : x and y. 

   compute vectors:

   vector1 = (b.x - a.x, b.y - a.y ) 

   vector2 = (y.x - x.x, y.y - x.y )

   compute lengths of the vectors:

   len_v1 = sqrt( vector1.x^2 + vector1.y^2 )

   len_v2 = sqrt( vector2.x^2 + vector2.y^2 )

   if abs( len_v2 - len_v1 ) > threshold_length (e.g. 0.5 ):

      continue (probably not a valid correspondance).

   now compute angle between vectors.

   if vector1 is length zero → skip.

   compute normalized vectors:

   n_v1 = (vector1.x / len_v1, vector1.y / len_v1 )

   n_v2 = (vector2.x / len_v2, vector2.y / len_v2 )

   compute the dot_product and cross_product.

   dot = n_v1.x * n_v2.x + n_v1.y * n_v2.y 

   cross = n_v1.x * n_v2.y - n_v1.y * n_v2.x 

   theta = math.atan2( cross, dot )

   # The rotation angle is theta. 

   # compute rotation matrix:

   R00 = cos(theta)

   R01 = -sin(theta)

   R10 = sin(theta)

   R11 = cos(theta)

   # Now, compute the translation T such that R*a + T = x ?

   # Let's compute T.x and T.y:

   T_x = x.x - ( R00 * a.x + R01 * a.y )

   T_y = x.y - ( R10 * a.x + R11 * a.y )

   So the translation vector T is (Tx, Ty).

   Thus, the transformation is R followed by T.

   Now, compute inliers: for all points in map1, after applying R and T, their transformed coordinates must be within threshold of some map2 point.

   count = count_inliers(R, T).

   (count is done via the grid method above.)

   if count is higher than the current best:

         save the R and T and count as new best.

Once all iterations done, select the best transformation, then:

- We can now apply the transformation on every point of map1 and map it to the nearest point in map2 (under the threshold). 

But how to determine the correspondence between individual points? Since the problem requires that each line outputs a pair (i,j) where i is from map1, j from map2.

Thus after finding R and T that give the best inlier count (> N), then:

Iterate over all points in map1: 

   For each point map1_pt:

       compute q = R * (map1_pt) + T 

       and find the closest point in map2 within threshold.

   — The closest point in map2 to q is the correspondance. 

However, multiple points in map1 may map to the same point in map2, or multiple points in map2 may be close to a given q. This would require some resolving.

Alternatively, we can use a reciprocal approach (matching is mutual).

Thus, the steps would be:

- For each point in map1 compute its corresponding q point (transformed).

- For each point in map2, compute whether it has a corresponding point from map1. 

This may use a second grid for map2's points or use KDtree for efficient NN search. 

Alternatively， using the grid:

Compute for all map1 points' transformed coordinates q. For each such q, find the closest map2 point within threshold and record their pairing, and then for each pair, increment a counter or mark as corresponding.

However, to construct the pairing between map1 and map2, this may not be error proof. There might be one to many mappings, so perhaps the best way is to collect all map1 to map2 pairs where the distance is within threshold, and make sure that each map1 and map2 point is paired once. But this requires choosing for each a unique pair such as picking the closest to each point.

Thus:

- For each map2_pt, find all the map1' s q points which are within threshold distance. 

- Then do a bipartite graph matching where we want to match map1 to map2 with one-to-one.

But this is computationally intensive.

Alternatively, the problem says:

 the pairs do not need to be all correct, but over 90% correct.

Hence, perhaps a greedy approach:

1. Compute for every point in map1 their transformed q = R*p1_pos + T.

2. For each q, query in the map2's grid for the closest point(s) in map2 and record pairs. 

3. To avoid double-counting, once a map2 point is matched (i.e, one map1 point's q. is closest to it), it can be marked as taken. 

4. Proceed until N pairs have been chosen (since the death stars are N in count).

Then select up to N of these with maximum possible, even if some are duplicates.

But to pick N distinct pairs.

The problem requires exactly N pairs. So need to choose N pairs that have the best matches.

Find all possible pairs between map1 and map2 that are within the transform's threshold.

Then greedily select the pairs to maximize the coverage:

For each map1 point's transformed q, find the closest map2 point within threshold. Assign if not taken, else next closest.

   <#sep#>import torch
import numpy as np
import torch.nn as nn
from torch.autograd import *


class ScaledDotProductWithBoxAttention(nn.Module):
    """
    Scaled dot-product attention with box
    """

    def __init__(self, d_model, d_k, d_v, h, dropout=0.1, comment=None):
        """
        :param d_model: Output dimensionality of the model
        :param d_k: Dimensionality of queries and keys
        :param d_v: Dimensionality of values
        :param h: Number of heads
        """
        super(ScaledDotProductWithBoxAttention, self).__init__()
        self.fc_q = nn.Linear(d_model, h * d_k)
        self.fc_k = nn.Linear(d_model, h * d_k)
        self.fc_v = nn.Linear(d_model, h * d_v)
        self.fc_o = nn.Linear(h * d_v, d_model)
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(dropout)
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.d_k = d_k
        self.d_v = d_v
        self.h = h
        self.init_weights()
        self.comment = comment

    def init_weights(self):
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.fc_q.weight)
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.fc_k.weight)
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.fc_v.weight)
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.fc_o.weight)
        nn.init.constant_(self.fc_q.bias, 0)
        nn.init.constant_(self.fc_k.bias, 0)
        nn.init.constant_(self.fc_v.bias, 0)
        nn.init.constant_(self.fc_o.bias, 0)

    def forward(self, queries, keys, values, box_relation_embed_matrix,
        attention_mask=None, attention_weights=None):
        """
        Computes
        :param queries: Queries (b_s, nq, d_model)
        :param keys: Keys (b_s, nk, d_model)
        :param values: Values (b_s, nk, d_model)
        :param attention_mask: Mask over attention values (b_s, h, nq, nk). True indicates masking.
        :param attention_weights: Multiplicative weights for attention values (b_s, h, nq, nk).
        :return:
        """
        b_s, nq = queries.shape[:2]
        nk = keys.shape[1]
        q = self.fc_q(queries).view(b_s, nq, self.h, self.d_k).permute(0, 2,
            1, 3)
        k = self.fc_k(keys).view(b_s, nk, self.h, self.d_k).permute(0, 2, 3, 1)
        v = self.fc_v(values).view(b_s, nk, self.h, self.d_v).permute(0, 2,
            1, 3)
        att = torch.matmul(q, k) / np.sqrt(self.d_k)
        if attention_weights is not None:
            att = att * attention_weights
        if attention_mask is not None:
            att = att.masked_fill(attention_mask, -np.inf)
        w_g = box_relation_embed_matrix
        w_a = att
        w_mn = torch.log(torch.clamp(w_g, min=1e-06)) + w_a
        w_mn = torch.softmax(w_mn, -1)
        att = self.dropout(w_mn)
        out = torch.matmul(att, v).permute(0, 2, 1, 3).contiguous().view(b_s,
            nq, self.h * self.d_v)
        out = self.fc_o(out)
        return out


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4,
        4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'d_model': 4, 'd_k': 4, 'd_v': 4, 'h': 4}]

Then greedily select the pairs to maximize the coverage:

For each map1 point's transformed q, find the closest map2 point within threshold. Assign if not taken, else next closest.

But this could leave some points without matches. Alternatively pick the closest match in a first come fashion.

Alternatively, what if we:

Loop over all map1 points:

   For each point in map1's list:

     compute the transformed coordinates q 

     search map2's grid and find the nearest point (closest distance within threshold).

     if found → add the indices of both as a pair → and remove the map2 point from map2 (so it can't be used again).

   But this may leave some map2 points unpaired and early p lines get better pairs.

Alternatively, perhaps the best approach is to prefer pairs where the distance is smallest, then pick the N pairs with the smallest distance, as long as each is unique.

Thus:

Collect all pairs as (d_index1, d_index2, distance).

Select the ones with smallest distance, and choose N, ensuring that each index1 and index2 are unique.

 But that would need to prevent duplicate indices. This can be done using a selection process:

Sort the list of candidate pairs (those within threshold) by their distance. Then iterate through them, marking which indices are already chosen, and taking the first N pairs that have unique indices.

Alternatively, the steps:

1. Find all candidate pairs:

   list = [  (d1, d2, dist) for d1 in map1_indices for map2_indices, calculate the distance of transformd map1 point to that map2 point, if under threshold ]

2. Sort this list in order of increasing distance.

3. Then pick the pairs from this list, taking the first N pairs where indices are not already used. Make sure no d1 or d2 are repeated.

   — this requires tracking map1 and map2 indices as assigned.

This would guarantee that the best distance pairs (closest) are selected first.

However, if there are not enough such pairs (perhaps due to errors), we might have to include. However, given that the RANSAC identified a transformation with N inliers (>=N?), we 'd have sufficient pairs.

Thus, the plan is:

Once the best transformation is identified, precompute all points in map1's transformed coordinates.

then, for each map1 point do a query for closest map2 point. 

But in code, to do this efficiently:

We need to:

- For each map1 index i:

compute q_i = R*(x,y) + T 

compute cell = floor(q_i.x/delta), floor(q_i.y/delta), then check nearby cells for map2 points.

for each map2 point in nearby cells:

compute distance squared between (qx, qy) and (px, py):

d_squared = (qx - px)^2 + (qy - py)^2?

we can store the closest map2 point index and distance.

then for all map1 points, store their closest map2 point (smallest d < threshold).

 collect all such pairs.

Then from this collection, choose the best N pairs to select as the answer, ensuring that each is unique.

This requires:

- A list of candidate map1-to-map2 pairs where distance is below threshold.

- Then select N pairs among them with best distance (smallest), and no duplicate.

Thus, even if the list has entries more than N, pick the first N with the minimal distance and no duplicates.

This seems manageable.

Thus, putting it all together:

Now, let's think about possible edge cases:

- Duplicate points between map1 and map2 (e.g., same coordinates are in both)

- Points that are noise in both maps but by chance align due to threshold and transformation.

These could be selected as false positives, but as long as less than 10% of the pairs are wrong, it's okay.

Testing on concrete examples would help.

Once the grid is built.

Then, in each RANSAC iteration:

def compute_R_T(a, b, x, y):

    vector1 = ( (b.x - a.x), (b.y - a.y) )

    vector2 = ( (y.x -x.x), (y.y - x.y) )

    len_v1_sq = vector1[0]**2 + vector1[1]**2 

    if len_v1_sq < 1e-6: # length almost zero, skip 

        return None 

    len_v1 = math.sqrt(len_v1_sq)

    len_v2 = math.hypot( vector2[0], vector2[1] )

    if abs(len_v2 - len_v1) > threshold_length: # say 0.5

        return None 

    # proceeding 

    # compute direction 

    n_v1_x = vector1[0]/len_v1 

    n_v1_y = vector1[1]/len_v1 

    n_v2_x = vector2[0]/len_v2 

    n_v2_y = vector2[1]/len_v2 

    dot = n_v1_x * n_v2_x + n_v1_y * n_v2_y 

    cross = n_v1_x * n_v2_y - n_v1_y * n_v2_x 

    theta = math.atan2(cross, dot )

    R00 = math.cos(theta)

    R01 = -math.sin(theta)

    R10 = math.sin(theta)

    R11 = math.cos(theta)

    T_x = x.x - ( R00* a.x + R01* a.y )

    T_y = x.y - ( R10* a.x + R11* a.y )

    return ( [[R00,R01], [R10,R11] ], (T_x, T_y) )

Then, when you have R and T (rotation and translation):

def get_inlier_count(R, T):

    R00,R01 = R[0][0], R[0][1]

    R10,R11 = R[1][0], R[1][1]

    tx, ty = T 

    count =0 

    for (a_x, a_y, a_idx) in map1: 

        # compute transformed coordinates 

        qx = R00 * a_x + R01 * a_y + tx 

        qy = R10 * a_x + R11 * a_y + ty 

        # find if any map2 point is within threshold 

        # search via grid 

        cx = int(math.floor(qx / delta ))

        cy = int(math.floor(qy / delta ))

        # search around 

        found = False 

        for dx in (-1,0,1):

            for dy in (-1,0,1):

                key_cell = ( cx +dx, cy+dy )

                if key_cell in map2_grid:

                    for (px, py, idx) in map2_grid[ key_cell ]: 

                        dx_dist = qx - px 

                        dy_dist = qy - py 

                        dist_sq = dx_dist**2 + dy_dist**2 

                        if dist_sq < threshold_sq: # threshold is 0.5, squared is 0.25

                            found = True 

                            break 

                        if found:

                            break 

                if found: 

                    count +=1 

                    break 

    return count

            v.List<#sep#>from torch.nn import Module
import torch
import numpy as np
import torch.nn as nn
from torch.autograd import *


class ScaledDotProductWithBoxAttention(nn.Module):
    """
    Scaled dot-product attention with box
    """

    def __init__(self, d_model, d_k, d_v, h, dropout=0.1, comment=None):
        """
        :param d_model: Output dimensionality of the model
        :param d_k: Dimensionality of queries and keys
        :param d_v: Dimensionality of values
        :param h: Number of heads
        """
        super(ScaledDotProductWithBoxAttention, self).__init__()
        self.fc_q = nn.Linear(d_model, h * d_k)
        self.fc_k = nn.Linear(d_model, h * d_k)
        self.fc_v = nn.Linear(d_model, h * d_v)
        self.fc_o = nn.Linear(h * d_v, d_model)
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(dropout)
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.d_k = d_k
        self.d_v = d_v
        self.h = h
        self.init_weights()
        self.comment = comment

    def init_weights(self):
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.fc_q.weight)
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.fc_k.weight)
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.fc_v.weight)
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.fc_o.weight)
        nn.init.constant_(self.fc_q.bias, 0)
        nn.init.constant_(self.fc_k.bias, 0)
        nn.init.constant_(self.fc_v.bias, 0)
        nn.init.constant_(self.fc_o.bias, 0)

    def forward(self, queries, keys, values, box_relation_embed_matrix,
        attention_mask=None, attention_weights=None):
        """
        Computes
        :param queries: Queries (b_s, nq, d_model)
        :param keys: Keys (b_s, nk, d_model)
        :param values: Values (b_s, nk, d_model)
        :param attention_mask: Mask over attention values (b_s, h, nq, nk). True indicates masking.
        :param attention_weights: Multiplicative weights for attention values (b_s, h, nq, nk).
        :return:
        """
        b_s, nq = queries.shape[:2]
        nk = keys.shape[1]
        q = self.fc_q(queries).view(b_s, nq, self.h, self.d_k).permute(0, 2,
            1, 3)
        k = self.fc_k(keys).view(b_s, nk, self.h, self.d_k).permute(0, 2, 3, 1)
        v = self.fc_v(values).view(b_s, nk, self.h, self.d_v).permute(0, 2,
            1, 3)
        att = torch.matmul(q, k) / np.sqrt(self.d_k)
        if attention_weights is not None:
            att = att * attention_weights
        if attention_mask is not None:
            att = att.masked_fill(attention_mask, -np.inf)
        w_g = box_relation_embed_matrix
        w_a = att
        w_mn = torch.log(torch.clamp(w_g, min=1e-06)) + w_a
        w_mn = torch.softmax(w_mn, -1)
        att = self.dropout(w_mn)
        out = torch.matmul(att, v).permute(0, 2, 1, 3).contiguous().view(b_s,
            nq, self.h * self.d_v)
        out = self.fc_o(out)
        return out


class MultiHeadBoxAttention(Module):
    """
    Multi-head attention layer with Dropout and Layer Normalization and box attention.
    """

    def __init__(self, d_model, d_k, d_v, h, dropout=0.1,
        identity_map_reordering=False, can_be_stateful=False,
        attention_module=None, attention_module_kwargs=None, comment=None):
        super(MultiHeadBoxAttention, self).__init__()
        self.identity_map_reordering = identity_map_reordering
        self.attention = ScaledDotProductWithBoxAttention(d_model=d_model,
            d_k=d_k, d_v=d_v, h=h, comment=comment)
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(p=dropout)
        self.layer_norm = nn.LayerNorm(d_model)
        self.can_be_stateful = can_be_stateful
        if self.can_be_stateful:
            self.register_state('running_keys', torch.zeros((0, d_model)))
            self.register_state('running_values', torch.zeros((0, d_model)))

    def forward(self, queries, keys, values, box_relation_embed_matrix,
        attention_mask=None, attention_weights=None):
        if self.can_be_stateful and self._is_stateful:
            self.running_keys = torch.cat([self.running_keys, keys], 1)
            keys = self.running_keys
            self.running_values = torch.cat([self.running_values, values], 1)
            values = self.running_values
        if self.identity_map_reordering:
            q_norm = self.layer_norm(queries)
            k_norm = self.layer_norm(keys)
            v_norm = self.layer_norm(values)
            out = self.attention(q_norm, k_norm, v_norm,
                box_relation_embed_matrix, attention_mask, attention_weights)
            out = queries + self.dropout(torch.relu(out))
        else:
            out = self.attention(queries, keys, values,
                box_relation_embed_matrix, attention_mask, attention_weights)
            out = self.dropout(out)
            out = self.layer_norm(queries + out)
        return out


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4,
        4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'d_model': 4, 'd_k': 4, 'd_v': 4, 'h': 4}]

    R10, R11 = R[1][0], R[1][1]

sepal_ui/sepalwidgets/alert.py:<#sep#>from torch.nn import Module
import torch
import numpy as np
import torch.nn as nn
from torch.autograd import *


class ScaledDotProductAttention(nn.Module):
    """
    Scaled dot-product attention
    """

    def __init__(self, d_model, d_k, d_v, h):
        """
        :param d_model: Output dimensionality of the model
        :param d_k: Dimensionality of queries and keys
        :param d_v: Dimensionality of values
        :param h: Number of heads
        """
        super(ScaledDotProductAttention, self).__init__()
        self.fc_q = nn.Linear(d_model, h * d_k)
        self.fc_k = nn.Linear(d_model, h * d_k)
        self.fc_v = nn.Linear(d_model, h * d_v)
        self.fc_o = nn.Linear(h * d_v, d_model)
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.d_k = d_k
        self.d_v = d_v
        self.h = h
        self.init_weights()

    def init_weights(self):
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.fc_q.weight)
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.fc_k.weight)
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.fc_v.weight)
        nn.init.xavier_uniform_(self.fc_o.weight)
        nn.init.constant_(self.fc_q.bias, 0)
        nn.init.constant_(self.fc_k.bias, 0)
        nn.init.constant_(self.fc_v.bias, 0)
        nn.init.constant_(self.fc_o.bias, 0)

    def forward(self, queries, keys, values, attention_mask=None,
        attention_weights=None):
        """
        Computes
        :param queries: Queries (b_s, nq, d_model)
        :param keys: Keys (b_s, nk, d_model)
        :param values: Values (b_s, nv, d_model)
        :param attention_mask: Mask over attention values (b_s, h, nq, nk). True indicates masking.
        :param attention_weights: Multiplicative weights for attention values (b_s, h, n_k, d_v)
        :return: out (b_s, nq, d_model)
        """
        b_s, nq = queries.shape[:2]
        nk = keys.shape[1]
        q = self.fc_q(queries).view(b_s, nq, self.h, self.d_k).permute(0, 2,
            1, 3)
        k = self.fc_k(keys).view(b_s, nk, self.h, self.d_k).permute(0, 2, 3, 1)
        v = self.fc_v(values).view(b_s, nk, self.h, self.d_v).permute(0, 2,
            1, 3)
        att = torch.matmul(q, k) / np.sqrt(self.d_k)
        if attention_weights is not None:
            att = att * attention_weights
        if attention_mask is not None:
            att = att.masked_fill(attention_mask, -np.inf)
        att = torch.softmax(att, -1)
        out = torch.matmul(att, v).permute(0, 2, 1, 3).contiguous().view(b_s,
            nq, self.h * self.d_v)
        out = self.fc_o(out)
        return out


class MultiHeadAttention(Module):
    """
    Multi-head attention layer with Dropout and Layer Normalization.
    """

    def __init__(self, d_model, d_k, d_v, h, dropout=0.1,
        identity_map_reordering=False, can_be_stateful=False,
        attention_module=None, attention_module_kwargs=None):
        super(MultiHeadAttention, self).__init__()
        self.identity_map_reordering = identity_map_reordering
        if attention_module is not None:
            if attention_module_kwargs is not None:
                self.attention = attention_module(d_model=d_model, d_k=d_k,
                    d_v=d_v, h=h, **attention_module_kwargs)
            else:
                self.attention = attention_module(d_model=d_model, d_k=d_k,
                    d_v=d_v, h=h)
        else:
            self.attention = ScaledDotProductAttention(d_model=d_model, d_k
                =d_k, d_v=d_v, h=h)
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(p=dropout)
        self.layer_norm = nn.LayerNorm(d_model)
        self.can_be_stateful = can_be_stateful
        if self.can_be_stateful:
            self.register_state('running_keys', torch.zeros((0, d_model)))
            self.register_state('running_values', torch.zeros((0, d_model)))

    def forward(self, queries, keys, values, attention_mask=None,
        attention_weights=None):
        if self.can_be_stateful and self._is_stateful:
            self.running_keys = torch.cat([self.running_keys, keys], 1)
            keys = self.running_keys
            self.running_values = torch.cat([self.running_values, values], 1)
            values = self.running_values
        if self.identity_map_reordering:
            q_norm = self.layer_norm(queries)
            k_norm = self.layer_norm(keys)
            v_norm = self.layer_norm(values)
            out = self.attention(q_norm, k_norm, v_norm, attention_mask,
                attention_weights)
            out = queries + self.dropout(torch.relu(out))
        else:
            out = self.attention(queries, keys, values, attention_mask,
                attention_weights)
            out = self.dropout(out)
            out = self.layer_norm(queries + out)
        return out


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4])
        ]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'d_model': 4, 'd_k': 4, 'd_v': 4, 'h': 4}]

(x + z x^{1/3})^3 ≥4x^{5/3}(1 + z^3 )

Divide both sides by x^{5/3} (assuming x>0):

( (x^{2/3} + z )^3 ) ≥4(1 + z^3 )

Let me set t =x^{2/3}, so that the left side becomes (t + z)^3 and the right side is 4(1 + z^3).

Thus, the inequality reduces to:

(t + z)^3 ≥4(1 + z^3), where t =x^{2/3}, and x>0, z = y/x^{1/3<#sep#>human: The recent volume of MAA's Mathematics Magazine had an article "Connecting the Dots: Maximal Polygons on a Square Grid" by Sam Chow, Ayla Gafni, and Paul Gafni about making (very convex) \$n^2\$-gons where each vertex is a different point of the \$n \times n\$ grid.

One is not allowed to have two consecutive segments be collinear, since that would merge two sides of the polygon.

For example, the following images are from Figures 2 and 10 in the Chow, Gafni, Gafni paper, which show a \$49\$-gon on the \$7 \times 7\$ grid of dots and a \$100\$-gon on the \$10 \times 10\$ grid of dots:
   

In the paper, the authors show that it is possible to form these \$n^2\$-gons for all integers \$n \geq 2\$ except for \$n=3\$ and \$n=5\$.

To score this, I will run the code on my machine—a 2017 MacBook with an Intel chip and 8GB of RAM—for five minutes with increasing sizes of \$n\$. Your score will be the maximum value of \$n\$ that your program can produce, and ties will be broken by the amount of time it took to get to that value.

    20	        args<#sep#>import torch
import numpy as np
import torch.nn as nn
from torch.nn.parameter import Parameter
import torch.nn.functional as F


class QREmbeddingBag(nn.Module):
    """Computes sums or means over two 'bags' of embeddings, one using the quotient
    of the indices and the other using the remainder of the indices, without
    instantiating the intermediate embeddings, then performs an operation to combine these.

    For bags of constant length and no :attr:`per_sample_weights`, this class

        * with ``mode="sum"`` is equivalent to :class:`~torch.nn.Embedding` followed by ``torch.sum(dim=0)``,
        * with ``mode="mean"`` is equivalent to :class:`~torch.nn.Embedding` followed by ``torch.mean(dim=0)``,
        * with ``mode="max"`` is equivalent to :class:`~torch.nn.Embedding` followed by ``torch.max(dim=0)``.

    However, :class:`~torch.nn.EmbeddingBag` is much more time and memory efficient than using a chain of these
    operations.

    QREmbeddingBag also supports per-sample weights as an argument to the forward
    pass. This scales the output of the Embedding before performing a weighted
    reduction as specified by ``mode``. If :attr:`per_sample_weights`` is passed, the
    only supported ``mode`` is ``"sum"``, which computes a weighted sum according to
    :attr:`per_sample_weights`.

    Known Issues:
    Autograd breaks with multiple GPUs. It breaks only with multiple embeddings.

    Args:
        num_categories (int): total number of unique categories. The input indices must be in
                              0, 1, ..., num_categories - 1.
        embedding_dim (list): list of sizes for each embedding vector in each table. If ``"add"``
                              or ``"mult"`` operation are used, these embedding dimensions must be
                              the same. If a single embedding_dim is used, then it will use this
                              embedding_dim for both embedding tables.
        num_collisions (int): number of collisions to enforce.
        operation (string, optional): ``"concat"``, ``"add"``, or ``"mult". Specifies the operation
                                      to compose embeddings. ``"concat"`` concatenates the embeddings,
                                      ``"add"`` sums the embeddings, and ``"mult"`` multiplies
                                      (component-wise) the embeddings.
                                      Default: ``"mult"``
        max_norm (float, optional): If given, each embedding vector with norm larger than :attr:`max_norm`
                                    is renormalized to have norm :attr:`max_norm`.
        norm_type (float, optional): The p of the p-norm to compute for the :attr:`max_norm` option. Default ``2``.
        scale_grad_by_freq (boolean, optional): if given, this will scale gradients by the inverse of frequency of
                                                the words in the mini-batch. Default ``False``.
                                                Note: this option is not supported when ``mode="max"``.
        mode (string, optional): ``"sum"``, ``"mean"`` or ``"max"``. Specifies the way to reduce the bag.
                                 ``"sum"`` computes the weighted sum, taking :attr:`per_sample_weights`
                                 into consideration. ``"mean"`` computes the average of the values
                                 in the bag, ``"max"`` computes the max value over each bag.
                                 Default: ``"mean"``
        sparse (bool, optional): if ``True``, gradient w.r.t. :attr:`weight` matrix will be a sparse tensor. See
                                 Notes for more details regarding sparse gradients. Note: this option is not
                                 supported when ``mode="max"``.

    Attributes:
        weight (Tensor): the learnable weights of each embedding table is the module of shape
                         `(num_embeddings, embedding_dim)` initialized using a uniform distribution
                         with sqrt(1 / num_categories).

    Inputs: :attr:`input` (LongTensor), :attr:`offsets` (LongTensor, optional), and
        :attr:`per_index_weights` (Tensor, optional)

        - If :attr:`input` is 2D of shape `(B, N)`,

          it will be treated as ``B`` bags (sequences) each of fixed length ``N``, and
          this will return ``B`` values aggregated in a way depending on the :attr:`mode`.
          :attr:`offsets` is ignored and required to be ``None`` in this case.

        - If :attr:`input` is 1D of shape `(N)`,

          it will be treated as a concatenation of multiple bags (sequences).
          :attr:`offsets` is required to be a 1D tensor containing the
          starting index positions of each bag in :attr:`input`. Therefore,
          for :attr:`offsets` of shape `(B)`, :attr:`input` will be viewed as
          having ``B`` bags. Empty bags (i.e., having 0-length) will have
          returned vectors filled by zeros.

        per_sample_weights (Tensor, optional): a tensor of float / double weights, or None
            to indicate all weights should be taken to be ``1``. If specified, :attr:`per_sample_weights`
            must have exactly the same shape as input and is treated as having the same
            :attr:`offsets`, if those are not ``None``. Only supported for ``mode='sum'``.


    Output shape: `(B, embedding_dim)`

    """
    __constants__ = ['num_categories', 'embedding_dim', 'num_collisions',
        'operation', 'max_norm', 'norm_type', 'scale_grad_by_freq', 'mode',
        'sparse']

    def __init__(self, num_categories, embedding_dim, num_collisions,
        operation='mult', max_norm=None, norm_type=2.0, scale_grad_by_freq=
        False, mode='mean', sparse=False, _weight=None):
        super(QREmbeddingBag, self).__init__()
        assert operation in ['concat', 'mult', 'add'], 'Not valid operation!'
        self.num_categories = num_categories
        if isinstance(embedding_dim, int) or len(embedding_dim) == 1:
            self.embedding_dim = [embedding_dim, embedding_dim]
        else:
            self.embedding_dim = embedding_dim
        self.num_collisions = num_collisions
        self.operation = operation
        self.max_norm = max_norm
        self.norm_type = norm_type
        self.scale_grad_by_freq = scale_grad_by_freq
        if self.operation == 'add' or self.operation == 'mult':
            assert self.embedding_dim[0] == self.embedding_dim[1
                ], 'Embedding dimensions do not match!'
        self.num_embeddings = [int(np.ceil(num_categories / num_collisions)
            ), num_collisions]
        if _weight is None:
            self.weight_q = Parameter(torch.Tensor(self.num_embeddings[0],
                self.embedding_dim[0]))
            self.weight_r = Parameter(torch.Tensor(self.num_embeddings[1],
                self.embedding_dim[1]))
            self.reset_parameters()
        else:
            assert list(_weight[0].shape) == [self.num_embeddings[0], self.
                embedding_dim[0]
                ], 'Shape of weight for quotient table does not match num_embeddings and embedding_dim'
            assert list(_weight[1].shape) == [self.num_embeddings[1], self.
                embedding_dim[1]
                ], 'Shape of weight for remainder table does not match num_embeddings and embedding_dim'
            self.weight_q = Parameter(_weight[0])
            self.weight_r = Parameter(_weight[1])
        self.mode = mode
        self.sparse = sparse

    def reset_parameters(self):
        nn.init.uniform_(self.weight_q, np.sqrt(1 / self.num_categories))
        nn.init.uniform_(self.weight_r, np.sqrt(1 / self.num_categories))

    def forward(self, input, offsets=None, per_sample_weights=None):
        input_q = (input / self.num_collisions).long()
        input_r = torch.remainder(input, self.num_collisions).long()
        embed_q = F.embedding_bag(input_q, self.weight_q, offsets, self.
            max_norm, self.norm_type, self.scale_grad_by_freq, self.mode,
            self.sparse, per_sample_weights)
        embed_r = F.embedding_bag(input_r, self.weight_r, offsets, self.
            max_norm, self.norm_type, self.scale_grad_by_freq, self.mode,
            self.sparse, per_sample_weights)
        if self.operation == 'concat':
            embed = torch.cat((embed_q, embed_r), dim=1)
        elif self.operation == 'add':
            embed = embed_q + embed_r
        elif self.operation == 'mult':
            embed = embed_q * embed_r
        return embed

    def extra_repr(self):
        s = '{num_embeddings}, {embedding_dim}'
        if self.max_norm is not<#sep#>3}(1 + z^3 )

     9	class Model(HasTraits):
    10	    """
    11	    Model object to store information of your computation (input, output.. etc)
    12	    The Model structure is based on traitlets and embed function for export or import in json format.
    13	    The model traintlets can be bind to any widget field.
    14	
    15	    """
    16	
    17	    def __repr__(self):
    18	        """Method to represent the Model objects as a string"""
    19	
    20	        args = ", ".join([f"{k}={v}" for k, v in self.export_data().items()]).strip()
    21	
    22	        return f"{self.__class__.__name__}({args})"
    23	
    24	    def export_data(self):
    25	        """
    26	        Export a json description of all the object traitlets
    27	        Note that the members will be ignored
    28	
    29	        Return:
    30	            (dict): the serialized traitlets
    31	        """
    32	
    33	        return self.__dict__["_trait_values"]
    34	
    35	    def import_data(self, data):
    36	        """
    37	        Import the model (i.e. the traitlets) from a json file
    38	
    39	        Params:
    40	            json (dict): the json ditionary of the model
    41	
    42	        Return:
    43	            self
    44	        """
    45	
    46	        if type(data) == str:
    47	            data = json.loads(data)
    48	
    49	        for k, v in data.items():
    50	            getattr(self, k)  # to raise an error if the json is malformed
    51	            setattr(self, k, v)

            max_norm, self.norm_type, self.scale_grad_by_freq, self.mode,

        if self.operation == 'concat':
            embed = torch.cat((embed_q, embed_r), dim=1)
        elif self.operation == 'add':
            embed = embed_q + embed_r
        elif self.operation == 'mult':
            embed = embed_q * embed_r
        return embed

    def extra_repr(self):
        s = '{num_embeddings}, {embedding_dim}'
        if self.max_norm is not None:
            s += ', max_norm={max_norm}'
        if self.norm_type != 2:
            s += ', norm_type={norm_type}'
        if self.scale_grad_by_freq is not False:
            s += ', scale_grad_by_freq={scale_grad_by_freq}'
        s += ', mode={mode}'
        return s.format(**self.__dict__)

from torch import nn


def get_EF(input_size, dim, method='learnable', head_dim=None, bias=True):
    """
    Retuns the E or F matrix, initialized via xavier initialization.
    This is the recommended way to do it according to the authors of the paper.
    Includes a method for convolution, as well as a method for no additional params.
    """
    assert method == 'learnable' or method == 'convolution' or method == 'no_params', "The method flag needs to be either 'learnable', 'convolution', or 'no_params'!"
    if method == 'convolution':
        conv = nn.Conv1d(head_dim, head_dim, kernel_size=int(input_size /
            dim), stride=int(input_size / dim))
        return conv
    if method == 'no_params':
        mat = torch.zeros((input_size, dim))
        torch.nn.init.normal_(mat, mean=0.0, std=1 / dim)
        return mat
    lin = nn.Linear(input_size, dim, bias)
    torch.nn.init.xavier_normal_(lin.weight)
    return lin


class linformerAttention(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, dim, dropout, input_size, dim_k=20, full_attention=
        False, parameter_sharing=None):
        super().__init__()
        self.dim = dim
        self.dropout = nn.Dropout(dropout)
        self.dim_k = dim_k
        self.full_attention = full_attention
        self.input_size = input_size
        self.print_dim = False
        self.E = get_EF(input_size, dim=self.dim_k, method='learnable',
            head_dim=self.dim)
        self.F = (get_EF(input_size, dim=self.dim_k, method='learnable',
            head_dim=self.dim) if parameter_sharing == 'none' or 
            parameter_sharing == 'headwise' else self.E)
        self.is_proj_tensor = isinstance(self.E, torch.Tensor)

    def forward(self, q, k, v):
        if self.print_dim:
            None
            None
            None
        if not self.full_attention:
            if self.is_proj_tensor:
                self.E = self.E
                b, h, *_ = q.shape
                projection_E = repeat(self.E, 'j d -> b h j d', b=b, h=h)
                k = torch.einsum('...di,...dj->...ij', k, projection_E)
            else:
                k = torch.einsum('...ij->...ji', k)
                k = self.E(k)
        if self.print_dim:
            None
            None
            None
        q = torch.einsum('...id,...dj->...ij', q, k)
        P_bar = q / torch.sqrt(torch.tensor(self.dim_k).type(q.type()))
        P_bar = P_bar.softmax(dim=-1)
        P_bar = self.dropout(P_bar)
        if not self.full_attention:
            if self.is_proj_tensor:
                self.F = self.F
                v = torch.matmul(v, self.F)
            else:
                v = torch.einsum('...ij->...ji', v)
                v = self.F(v)
        out = torch.einsum('...id,...jd->...ij', P_bar, v)
        return out


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand(
        [4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'dim': 4, 'dropout': 0.5, 'input_size': 4}]

**Final Answer**
\boxed{12}
</think>Given \(a, b, c > 0\) and \(abc = 1\), we need to find the minimum value of the expression:
\[
S = \frac{(a^3 + b^2c)^3}{a^5 + b^4c} + \frac{(b^3 + c^2a)^3}{b^5 + c^4a} + \frac{(c^3 + a^2b)^3}{c^5 + a^4b}
\]

First, we test the symmetric case where \(a = b = c = 1\):

\[
S = \frac{(1^3 + 1^2 \cdot 1)^3}{1^5 + 1^4 \cdot 1} + \frac{(1^3 + 1^2 \cdot 1)^3}{1^5 + 1^4 \cdot 1} + \frac{(1^3 + 1^2 \cdot 1)^3}{1^5 + 1^4 \cdot 1}
\]

Each term simplifies to:

\[
\frac{(1 + 1)^3}{1 + 1} = \frac{8}{2} = 4
\]

Thus, the sum of the three terms is:

\[
S = 4 + 4 + 4 = 12
\]

Next, we need to verify if this is indeed the minimum value. We consider the symmetry and the behavior of the expression under small perturbations around \(a = b = c = 1\).

Consider a small perturbation \(a = 1 + \epsilon\), \(b = 1\), and \(c = \frac{1}{1 + \epsilon}\) for a small \(\epsilon\). We calculate the first term \(T_1\):

\[
T_1 = \frac{((1+\epsilon)^3 + 1^2 \cdot \frac{1}{1+\epsilon})^3}{(1+\epsilon)^5 + 1^4 \cdot \frac{1}{1+\epsilon}}
\]

Expanding and simplifying for small \(\epsilon\):

\[
(1+\epsilon)^3 \approx 1 + 3\epsilon + 3\epsilon^2 + \epsilon^3
\]
\[
\frac{1}{1+\epsilon} \approx 1 - \epsilon + \epsilon^2
\]

Thus,

\[
(1+\epsilon)^3 + \frac{1}{1+\epsilon} \approx (1 + 3\epsilon + 3\epsilon^2 + \epsilon^3) + (1 - \epsilon + \epsilon^2) = 2 + 2\epsilon + 4\epsilon^2 + \epsilon^3
\]

Cubing this expression:

\[
(2 + 2\epsilon + 4\epsilon^2 + \epsilon^3)^3 \approx 8 + 24\epsilon + 72\epsilon^2 + \text{higher order terms}
\]

For the denominator:

\[
(1+\epsilon)^5 \approx 1 + 5\epsilon + 10\epsilon^2 + 10\epsilon^3 + 5\epsilon^4 + \epsilon^5
\]
\[
\frac{1}{1+\epsilon} \approx 1 - \epsilon + \epsilon^2
\]

Thus,

\[
(1+\epsilon)^5 + \frac{1}{1+\epsilon} \approx (1 + 5\epsilon + 10\epsilon^2 + 10\epsilon^3 + 5\epsilon^4 + \epsilon^5) + (1 - \epsilon + \epsilon^2) = 2 + 4\epsilon + 11\epsilon^2 + \text{higher order terms}
\]

The first term \(T_1\) becomes:

\[
T_1 \approx \frac{8 + 24\epsilon + 72\epsilon^2}{2 + 4\epsilon + 11\epsilon^2} \approx 4 + \text{higher order terms}
\]

Similarly, the other terms \(T_2\) and \(T_3\) will also be close to 4 when \(\epsilon\) is small. Therefore, \(S\) remains close to 12.

Given the symmetry and the behavior under small perturbations, we conclude that the minimum value of \(S\) is indeed achieved when \(a = b = c = 1\).

Thus, the minimum value of \(S\) is:

\[

from torch import nn
from torch.nn import functional as F
from torch import distributed as dist
import torch.distributed.nn


def gather_features(image_features, text_features, local_loss=False,
    gather_with_grad=False, rank=0, world_size=1, use_horovod=False):
    if use_horovod:
        assert hvd is not None, 'Please install horovod'
        if gather_with_grad:
            all_image_features = hvd.allgather(image_features)
            all_text_features = hvd.allgather(text_features)
        else:
            with torch.no_grad():
                all_image_features = hvd.allgather(image_features)
                all_text_features = hvd.allgather(text_features)
            if not local_loss:
                gathered_image_features = list(all_image_features.chunk(
                    world_size, dim=0))
                gathered_text_features = list(all_text_features.chunk(
                    world_size, dim=0))
                gathered_image_features[rank] = image_features
                gathered_text_features[rank] = text_features
                all_image_features = torch.cat(gathered_image_features, dim=0)
                all_text_features = torch.cat(gathered_text_features, dim=0)
    elif gather_with_grad:
        all_image_features = torch.cat(torch.distributed.nn.all_gather(
            image_features), dim=0)
        all_text_features = torch.cat(torch.distributed.nn.all_gather(
            text_features), dim=0)
    else:
        gathered_image_features = [torch.zeros_like(image_features) for _ in
            range(world_size)]
        gathered_text_features = [torch.zeros_like(text_features) for _ in
            range(world_size)]
        dist.all_gather(gathered_image_features, image_features)
        dist.all_gather(gathered_text_features, text_features)
        if not local_loss:
            gathered_image_features[rank] = image_features
            gathered_text_features[rank] = text_features
        all_image_features = torch.cat(gathered_image_features, dim=0)
        all_text_features = torch.cat(gathered_text_features, dim=0)
    return all_image_features, all_text_features


class ClipLoss(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, local_loss=False, gather_with_grad=False,
        cache_labels=False, rank=0, world_size=1, use_horovod=False):
        super().__init__()
        self.local_loss = local_loss
        self.gather_with_grad = gather_with_grad
        self.cache_labels = cache_labels
        self.rank = rank
        self.world_size = world_size
        self.use_horovod = use_horovod
        self.prev_num_logits = 0
        self.labels = {}

    def forward(self, image_features, text_features, logit_scale):
        device = image_features.device
        if self.world_size > 1:
            all_image_features, all_text_features = gather_features(
                image_features, text_features, self.local_loss, self.
                gather_with_grad, self.rank, self.world_size, self.use_horovod)
            if self.local_loss:
                logits_per_image = (logit_scale * image_features @
                    all_text_features.T)
                logits_per_text = (logit_scale * text_features @
                    all_image_features.T)
            else:
                logits_per_image = (logit_scale * all_image_features @
                    all_text_features.T)
                logits_per_text = logits_per_image.T
        else:
            logits_per_image = logit_scale * image_features @ text_features.T
            logits_per_text = logit_scale * text_features @ image_features.T
        num_logits = logits_per_image.shape[0]
        if self.prev_num_logits != num_logits or device not in self.labels:
            labels = torch.arange(num_logits, device=device, dtype=torch.long)
            if self.world_size > 1 and self.local_loss:
                labels = labels + num_logits * self.rank
            if self.cache_labels:
                self.labels[device] = labels
                self.prev_num_logits = num_logits
        else:
            labels = self.labels[device]
        total_loss = (F.cross_entropy(logits_per_image, labels) + F.
            cross_entropy(logits_per_text, labels)) / 2
        return total_loss


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]

First, y = cot x is the cotangent function. I remember cot x is cos x over sin x, right? So it has vertical asymptotes where sin x is zero, which is at x = nπ for any integer n. The cotangent function has a period of π, so it repeats every π units. Between each pair of asymptotes, the cotangent curve decreases from positive infinity to negative infinity. So, it's positive in (0, π), negative in (π, 2π), and so on.

The other curve is x² + y² - 2πx -15π² = 0. Let me rearrange this equation to recognize its shape. Let's complete the square for the x terms. 

Starting with x² - 2πx + y² = 15π². To complete the square for x, take the coefficient of x, which is -2π, divide by 2 to get -π, square it to get π². So, add and subtract π²:

(x² - 2πx + π²) + y² = 15π² + π²

That simplifies to (x - π)² + y² = 16π². Ah, so this is a circle with center at (π, 0) and radius 4π, because 16π² is (4π)².

Mathematically, two vectors are colinear if their cross product is zero. 

The cross product of the previous direction and new direction is (dx_prev * dy_new - dy_prev * dx_new). If this is zero, then they are colinear. 

So, to enforce that the new direction is not colinear with previous, we must ensure that cross product is not zero. 

Thus, when choosing next cell to move to, check (dx_prev * dy_new - dy_prev * dx_new) ≠ 0. 

So, when backtracking:

Previous direction (dx_prev, dy_prev)

Visited set (bitmask or other)

- Not visited yet.

- The direction vector from (x,y) to (nx, ny) must have a cross product with (dx_prev, dy_prev) non-zero.

At each step after the first, we must enforce that the next direction is not colinear with previous direction. 

Therefore, in backtracking, the state will require previous direction and current position and visited.

Possibly also, the path length so far (number of points visited). 

Also, to form a cycle, the last move must return to the starting point. 

Thus, the algorithm steps would be as follows:

Initialize starting at (0,0).

State is:

- (x,y) current position.

- (dx_prev, dy_prev) previous direction vector. 

- visited (set or bitmask).

- steps count (number of vertices visited so far, which is the path length).

Proceed recursively:

For each possible next unvisited point (nx, ny):

- Compute the direction to the new point: (dx = nx - x, dy = ny - y).

- If first step (steps == 2, since starting at step 1 is the starting node, etc.), don't need to check? Wait:

Wait steps count starts at 1 (current is first step is (0,0)). So moving to first actual step is steps = 2. 

Wait let's think:

At start, path has length 1 (only (0,0)).

From (0,0), first move is step 1 (moving to first other point). 

Now after moving, the path length is 2.

The next move (step 2) must ensure that the direction from previous is okay with the first direction. 

At that point, we have three points (0.0), p1, p2. So their three points mustn't be colinear. 

To enforce this, the direction from p1 to p2 must not be colinear with the direction from (0,0) to p1. 

So for step 2, the cross product between ( (dx_p1, dy_p1) ) (the first move's direction) and (dx_next, dy_next) (the new direction) must not be zero. 

Thus, for all steps except the first (the initial move), we check the direction of the next edge to not be colinear with previous edge's direction. 

So the algorithm is something like this:

Define a function backtrack(x, y, dx_prev, dy_prev, visited, step_count):

    if visited has all bits set (all points visited except for the last back to start):

        check whether the final step can return to start:

        compute direction from current (x,y) to start (0,0). 

        ensure this direction is not colinear with previous direction.

        if all then:

            if the returned direction's cross product with previous direction is not zero.

            then, return 1 or add to count (but need to account for rotation, etc?)

    else:

        for each unvisited (nx, ny):

            check if moving to (nx, ny) is possible:

            compute new dx = nx - x, new dy = ny - y.

            if step_count > 1 (i.e., not first step):

                if (dx_prev * new_dy - dy_prev * new_dx) == 0: 

                    skip this direction (this is colinear)

            add (nx, ny) to visited and recurse:

                backtrack(nx, ny, new_dx, new_dy, visited + (nx,ny), step_count+1)

        sum all the recursive results.

Now, counts this by backtracking all possible moves, but with pruning. 

However, the problem is that the original question requires counting equivalence classes up to rotation and reflection. 

Thus, this backtracking counts paths starting at (0,0), but with different orientations, so we are overcounting. 

So the algorithm above gives the number of polygons with a starting point at (0,0) and starting with whatever direction. However, the problem requires essentially different polygons modulo symmetries. 

Therefore, this approach counts everything including symmetries, so unless we enforce further restrictions, like the starting direction, we can't just sum. 

To account for the symmetries, here are some options:

Option 1: Fix the path's features such that we only count one polygon per equivalence class. 

To do this, we can impose constraints on the path which uniquely identify one representative from each class. 

For instance, among all paths in an equivalence class under the dihedral group, choose the lexicographically smallest path. 

But how? 

Alternatively, fix the starting point to (0,0) as done, but then impose ordering constraints on the direction taken by the first step. 

For example, require that the first step move to the smallest possible adjacent point (but perhaps not adjacent? ).

 Or, require that the path has a canonical form such that it it's rotated/ reflected to position where its starting point is (0,0), and then it's first step is minimal in some way. 

Alternatively, enforce that among all possible symmetries, the first move is in a particular direction (like up or right), whichever is lex smaller between (x,y) to (x+1,y) or (x,y+1).

It is designed on top of the amazing `ipyvuetify <https://ipyvuetify.readthedocs.io/en/latest/introduction.html>`_ library and will help developer to easily create interface for their workflows.

    71	How to commit
    72	-------------
    73	
    74	In this repository we use the Conventional Commits specification.
    75	The Conventional Commits specification is a lightweight convention on top of commit messages. It provides an easy set of rules for creating an explicit commit history; which makes it easier to write automated tools on top of. This convention dovetails with SemVer, by describing the features, fixes, and breaking changes made in commit messages.
    76	
    77	You can learn more about Conventional Commits following this `link <https://www.conventionalcommits.org/en/v1.0.0/>`_
    78	
    79	What can I push and where
    80	-------------------------
    81	
    82	Our branching system embed some rules to avoid crash of the production environment. If you want to contribute to this framework, here are some basic rules that we try our best to follow :
    83	
    84	-   the modification you offer is solving a critical bug in prod : **PR in hotfix**
    85	-   the modification you propose solve the following issues : test, documentation, typo, quality, refactoring, translation **PR in master**
    86	-   the modification you propose is a new feature : open an issue to discuss with the maintainers and then **PR to develop**
    87	
    88	the maintainers will try their best to use PR for new features, to help the community follow the development, for other modification they will simply push to the appropriate branch

import torch.nn as nn
import torch.cuda
import torch.distributed


class PositionwiseFeedForward(nn.Module):
    """ A two-layer Feed-Forward-Network with residual layer norm.

    Args:
        d_model (int): the size of input for the first-layer of the FFN.
        d_ff (int): the hidden layer size of the second-layer
            of the FNN.
        dropout (float): dropout probability in :math:`[0, 1)`.
    """

    def __init__(self, d_model, d_ff, dropout=0.1):
        super(PositionwiseFeedForward, self).__init__()
        self.w_1 = nn.Linear(d_model, d_ff)
        self.w_2 = nn.Linear(d_ff, d_model)
        self.layer_norm = nn.LayerNorm(d_model, eps=1e-06)
        self.dropout_1 = nn.Dropout(dropout)
        self.relu = nn.ReLU()
        self.dropout_2 = nn.Dropout(dropout)

    def forward(self, x):
        """Layer definition.

        Args:
            x: ``(batch_size, input_len, model_dim)``

        Returns:
            (FloatTensor): Output ``(batch_size, input_len, model_dim)``.
        """
        inter = self.dropout_1(self.relu(self.w_1(self.layer_norm(x))))
        output = self.dropout_2(self.w_2(inter))
        return output + x

    def update_dropout(self, dropout):
        self.dropout_1.p = dropout
        self.dropout_2.p = dropout


class AverageAttention(nn.Module):
    """
    Average Attention module from
    "Accelerating Neural Transformer via an Average Attention Network"
    :cite:`DBLP:journals/corr/abs-1805-00631`.

    Args:
       model_dim (int): the dimension of keys/values/queries,
           must be divisible by head_count
       dropout (float): dropout parameter
    """

    def __init__(self, model_dim, dropout=0.1, aan_useffn=False):
        self.model_dim = model_dim
        self.aan_useffn = aan_useffn
        super(AverageAttention, self).__init__()
        if aan_useffn:
            self.average_layer = PositionwiseFeedForward(model_dim,
                model_dim, dropout)
        self.gating_layer = nn.Linear(model_dim * 2, model_dim * 2)

    def cumulative_average_mask(self, batch_size, inputs_len, device):
        """
        Builds the mask to compute the cumulative average as described in
        :cite:`DBLP:journals/corr/abs-1805-00631` -- Figure 3

        Args:
            batch_size (int): batch size
            inputs_len (int): length of the inputs

        Returns:
            (FloatTensor):

            * A Tensor of shape ``(batch_size, input_len, input_len)``
        """
        triangle = torch.tril(torch.ones(inputs_len, inputs_len, dtype=
            torch.float, device=device))
        weights = torch.ones(1, inputs_len, dtype=torch.float, device=device
            ) / torch.arange(1, inputs_len + 1, dtype=torch.float, device=
            device)
        mask = triangle * weights.transpose(0, 1)
        return mask.unsqueeze(0).expand(batch_size, inputs_len, inputs_len)

    def cumulative_average(self, inputs, mask_or_step, layer_cache=None,
        step=None):
        """
        Computes the cumulative average as described in
        :cite:`DBLP:journals/corr/abs-1805-00631` -- Equations (1) (5) (6)

        Args:
            inputs (FloatTensor): sequence to average
                ``(batch_size, input_len, dimension)``
            mask_or_step: if cache is set, this is assumed
                to be the current step of the
                dynamic decoding. Otherwise, it is the mask matrix
                used to compute the cumulative average.
            layer_cache: a dictionary containing the cumulative average
                of the previous step.

        Returns:
            a tensor of the same shape and type as ``inputs``.
        """
        if layer_cache is not None:
            step = mask_or_step
            average_attention = (inputs + step * layer_cache['prev_g']) / (step
                 + 1)
            layer_cache['prev_g'] = average_attention
            return average_attention
        else:
            mask = mask_or_step
            return torch.matmul(mask, inputs)

    def forward(self, inputs, mask=None, layer_cache=None, step=None):
        """
        Args:
            inputs (FloatTensor): ``(batch_size, input_len, model_dim)``

        Returns:
            (FloatTensor, FloatTensor):

            * gating_outputs ``(batch_size, input_len, model_dim)``
            * average_outputs average attention
                ``(batch_size, input_len, model_dim)``
        """
        batch_size = inputs.size(0)
        inputs_len = inputs.size(1)
        average_outputs = self.cumulative_average(inputs, self.
            cumulative_average_mask(batch_size, inputs_len, inputs.device) if
            layer_cache is None else step, layer_cache=layer_cache)
        if self.aan_useffn:
            average_outputs = self.average_layer(average_outputs)
        gating_outputs = self.gating_layer(torch.cat((inputs,
            average_outputs), -1))
        input_gate, forget_gate = torch.chunk(gating_outputs, 2, dim=2)
        gating_outputs = torch.sigmoid(input_gate) * inputs + torch.sigmoid(
            forget_gate) * average_outputs
        return gating_outputs, average_outputs


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'model_dim': 4}]
