<#sep#>import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F
import torch.cuda
import torch.distributed


def aeq(*args):
    """
    Assert all arguments have the same value
    """
    arguments = (arg for arg in args)
    first = next(arguments)
    assert all(arg == first for arg in arguments
        ), 'Not all arguments have the same value: ' + str(args)


def sequence_mask(lengths, max_len=None):
    """
    Creates a boolean mask from sequence lengths.
    """
    batch_size = lengths.numel()
    max_len = max_len or lengths.max()
    return torch.arange(0, max_len, device=lengths.device).type_as(lengths
        ).repeat(batch_size, 1).lt(lengths.unsqueeze(1))


class GlobalAttention(nn.Module):
    """
    Global attention takes a matrix and a query vector. It
    then computes a parameterized convex combination of the matrix
    based on the input query.

    Constructs a unit mapping a query `q` of size `dim`
    and a source matrix `H` of size `n x dim`, to an output
    of size `dim`.


    .. mermaid::

       graph BT
          A[Query]
          subgraph RNN
            C[H 1]
            D[H 2]
            E[H N]
          end
          F[Attn]
          G[Output]
          A --> F
          C --> F
          D --> F
          E --> F
          C -.-> G
          D -.-> G
          E -.-> G
          F --> G

    All models compute the output as
    :math:`c = \\sum_{j=1}^{\\text{SeqLength}} a_j H_j` where
    :math:`a_j` is the softmax of a score function.
    Then then apply a projection layer to [q, c].

    However they
    differ on how they compute the attention score.

    * Luong Attention (dot, general):
       * dot: :math:`\\text{score}(H_j,q) = H_j^T q`
       * general: :math:`\\text{score}(H_j, q) = H_j^T W_a q`


    * Bahdanau Attention (mlp):
       * :math:`\\text{score}(H_j, q) = v_a^T \\text{tanh}(W_a q + U_a h_j)`


    Args:
       dim (int): dimensionality of query and key
       coverage (bool): use coverage term
       attn_type (str): type of attention to use, options [dot,general,mlp]
       attn_func (str): attention function to use, options [softmax,sparsemax]

    """

    def __init__(self, dim, coverage=False, attn_type='dot', attn_func=
        'softmax'):
        super(GlobalAttention, self).__init__()
        self.dim = dim
        assert attn_type in ['dot', 'general', 'mlp'
            ], 'Please select a valid attention type (got {:s}).'.format(
            attn_type)
        self.attn_type = attn_type
        assert attn_func in ['softmax', 'sparsemax'
            ], 'Please select a valid attention function.'
        self.attn_func = attn_func
        if self.attn_type == 'general':
            self.linear_in = nn.Linear(dim, dim, bias=False)
        elif self.attn_type == 'mlp':
            self.linear_context = nn.Linear(dim, dim, bias=False)
            self.linear_query = nn.Linear(dim, dim, bias=True)
            self.v = nn.Linear(dim, 1, bias=False)
        out_bias = self.attn_type == 'mlp'
        self.linear_out = nn.Linear(dim * 2, dim, bias=out_bias)
        if coverage:
            self.linear_cover = nn.Linear(1, dim, bias=False)

    def score(self, h_t, h_s):
        """
        Args:
          h_t (FloatTensor): sequence of queries ``(batch, tgt_len, dim)``
          h_s (FloatTensor): sequence of sources ``(batch, src_len, dim``

        Returns:
          FloatTensor: raw attention scores (unnormalized) for each src index
            ``(batch, tgt_len, src_len)``
        """
        src_batch, src_len, src_dim = h_s.size()
        tgt_batch, tgt_len, tgt_dim = h_t.size()
        aeq(src_batch, tgt_batch)
        aeq(src_dim, tgt_dim)
        aeq(self.dim, src_dim)
        if self.attn_type in ['general', 'dot']:
            if self.attn_type == 'general':
                h_t_ = h_t.view(tgt_batch * tgt_len, tgt_dim)
                h_t_ = self.linear_in(h_t_)
                h_t = h_t_.view(tgt_batch, tgt_len, tgt_dim)
            h_s_ = h_s.transpose(1, 2)
            return torch.bmm(h_t, h_s_)
        else:
            dim = self.dim
            wq = self.linear_query(h_t.view(-1, dim))
            wq = wq.view(tgt_batch, tgt_len, 1, dim)
            wq = wq.expand(tgt_batch, tgt_len, src_len, dim)
            uh = self.linear_context(h_s.contiguous().view(-1, dim))
            uh = uh.view(src_batch, 1, src_len, dim)
            uh = uh.expand(src_batch, tgt_len, src_len, dim)
            wquh = torch.tanh(wq + uh)
            return self.v(wquh.view(-1, dim)).view(tgt_batch, tgt_len, src_len)

    def forward(self, source, memory_bank, memory_lengths=None, coverage=None):
        """

        Args:
          source (FloatTensor): query vectors ``(batch, tgt_len, dim)``
          memory_bank (FloatTensor): source vectors ``(batch, src_len, dim)``
          memory_lengths (LongTensor): the source context lengths ``(batch,)``
          coverage (FloatTensor): None (not supported yet)

        Returns:
          (FloatTensor, FloatTensor):

          * Computed vector ``(tgt_len, batch, dim)``
          * Attention distribtutions for each query
            ``(tgt_len, batch, src_len)``
        """
        if source.dim() == 2:
            one_step = True
            source = source.unsqueeze(1)
        else:
            one_step = False
        batch, source_l, dim = memory_bank.size()
        batch_, target_l, dim_ = source.size()
        aeq(batch, batch_)
        aeq(dim, dim_)
        aeq(self.dim, dim)
        if coverage is not None:
            batch_, source_l_ = coverage.size()
            aeq(batch, batch_)
            aeq(source_l, source_l_)
        if coverage is not None:
            cover = coverage.view(-1).unsqueeze(1)
            memory_bank += self.linear_cover(cover).view_as(memory_bank)
            memory_bank = torch.tanh(memory_bank)
        align = self.score(source, memory_bank)
        if memory_lengths is not None:
            mask = sequence_mask(memory_lengths, max_len=align.size(-1))
            mask = mask.unsqueeze(1)
            align.masked_fill_(~mask, -float('inf'))
        if self.attn_func == 'softmax':
            align_vectors = F.softmax(align.view(batch * target_l, source_l
                ), -1)
        else:
            align_vectors = sparsemax(align.view(batch * target_l, source_l
                ), -1)
        align_vectors = align_vectors.view(batch, target_l, source_l)
        c = torch.bmm(align_vectors, memory_bank)
        concat_c = torch.cat([c, source], 2).view(batch * target_l, dim * 2)
        attn_h = self.linear_out(concat_c).view(batch, target_l, dim)
        if self.attn_type in ['general', 'dot']:
            attn_h = torch.tanh(attn_h)
        if one_step:
            attn_h = attn_h.squeeze(1)
            align_vectors = align_vectors.squeeze(1)
            batch_, dim_ = attn_h.size()
            aeq(batch, batch_)
            aeq(dim, dim_)
            batch_, source_l_ = align_vectors.size()
            aeq(batch, batch_)
            aeq(source_l, source_l_)
        else:
            attn_h = attn_h.transpose(0, 1).contiguous()
            align_vectors = align_vectors.transpose(0, 1).contiguous()
            target_l_, batch_, dim_ = attn_h.size()
            aeq(target_l, target_l_)
            aeq(batch, batch_)
            aeq(dim, dim_)
            target_l_, batch_, source_l_ = align_vectors.size()
            aeq(target_l, target_l_)
            aeq(batch, batch_)
            aeq(source_l, source_l_)
        return attn_h, align<#sep#>), cot²t behaves the same way as in (0, π): starts at +infty, decreases to zero at t = 3π/2 (s = π/2), then increases to +infty at t = 2π. The term t² in (π, 2π) ranges from π² to 4π². So f(t) = t² + cot²t - 16π².

Hmm, so that approach can't be used to count the number of equivalence classes. It just gives a sample path. 

Thus, this requires another strategy. 

Alternative approach: using memoization and symmetry-breaking constraints. 

In backtracking, impose constraints:

- Start from (0, 0). 

- The first edge must be the minimal possible (e.g., going to (0, 1)). 

- The second move must be to a next point that forms the minimal lexicographical path. 

This could reduce symmetries by fixing the initial turns. 

But for large n, the branching factor may still be prohibitive. 

Perhaps the maximum n is small. 

But getting back to code.

Perhaps the code will need to backtracking with bitmask for visited, tracking current position, path length, and previous direction. 

Additionally, to count equivalence classes, the code could use canonical form for the entire path. 

For example, any path can be rotated/reflected to a representative where the first move form (0,0) to (0,1). 

Thus, the code can count all paths that start with (0,0) and move next to (0,1), and which other constraints enforce that no rotation/reflection can have worse direction. 

Thus, by fixing the first two moves to a particular direction, we count 1/ (the number of symmetries) * total number? Well, not exactly. 

Perhaps the overlap between symmetry classes causes this to count some paths but discard others. 

Alternatively, fix the starting vertex at (0,0), and first move to (0,1), but allow any other next steps. 

This way, all symmetric polygons would have a representant in this search space. 

But many equivalent polygons will have different first moves so this may not be the case. 

Alternatively, count all such paths starting with (0,0), then divide by the number of symmetries, but accounting for overcounting. 

This is where Burnside's lemma is useful, but computing the fixees of rotations/reflections is still needed.

First, let's think of how to represent the visited set: 

For n=4 (4x4 board):

16 points, so we can represent the visited as an integer of 16 bits. 

Each step, the function can track current position (x,y), previous direction (dx, dy) or none (for the first step), and visited bitmask. 

The next positions are all unvisited points. 

But even for n=4, the first step can choose any of 15 points, which a lot. 

Wait but with the constraints of the second step's direction needing to he non-colinear with the first. 

Hm, but perhaps the code will need to he recursive and use memoization for visited, position, previous direction.

So let me think how to implement this in code.

First, for a given n, we can represent the grid squares as coordinates (x,y), where x and y range from 0 to n-1. 

The bitmask would be an integer where each bit indicates whether a coordinate is visited. 

The visited mask can be represented as follows (for n=4):

for each point (a,b), it corresponds to (4 a +b). Thus, the bitmask can have positions from 0 to 15 for a 4x4 board. 

The starting point is (0,0) which corresponds to index 0. So the bitmask starts with a 1<<0 set. 

Wait but the bitmask must track all visited points. So for n=4, each point (x,y) is mapped to (x*n + y), so for (0,0) it's 0, (0,1)=1, ..., (3,0)=12, (3,3)=15. 

Thus bitmask is 1<<0 initially. 

The base case: after visiting all n×n points, the last move must connect back to (0,0), and the direction of this step must not be colinear with the previous move's direction. 

So the algorithm:

The backtracking function could look like this:

import sys

n = # the current n we're testing.

results = 0

def backtrack(x, y, dx_prev, dy_prev, visited, step_count):

    global results

    if step_count == n*n:

        # We need to close the polygon.

        # The next move must be to (0,0), so check if that is unvisited? Wait no: all have been visited except that we're at current (x,y), and starting is part of the visited.

        # Wait after visiting all points except the first, the last step must move back to (0,0).

        # Thus, from current position (x,y), must check whether (0,0) is unvisited? Wait no. Since all are visited, so visited has all bits except possibly didn't complete.

        # Wait step_count is the number of steps taken. If step_count equals n², that means we have visited all points exactly once. Thus the current position is the nth point, and need to close the cycle to (0,0).

        # Thus, check that (dx, dy) from current to (0,0) is not colinear with previous direction (dx_prev, dy_prev)

        dx_circle = 0 - x

        dy_circle = 0 - y

        cross = dx_prev*dy_circle - dy_prev*dx_circle

        if cross ==0:

            return

        # also ensure that the movement from current to 0,0 is valid as the final step

        # but it's compulsory to complete the clycle

        # also must not visit any points again.

        # Since all points are already visited, except the start, which is already counted.

        # So we can consider this as a valid END.

        results +=1

        return

    for nx in 0 to n-1:

        for ny in 0 to n-1:

            if (nx << x) | ( ny << y) in visited? no. If bitmask has bit set for (nx, ny) then continue.

            # need to get the index of (nx, ny):

            index = nx*n + ny

            if visited & (1 << index) !=0:

                continue

            new_dx= nx -x

            new_dy= ny- y

            # check if this move is allowed.

            # if step_count >=2 (i.e., there was a previous direction):

            if step_count >=2:

                cross_val = dx_prev * new_dy - dy_prev * new_dx

                if cross_val ==0:

                    continue

            # now check if the next direction is eligible.

            # then, proceed with next step.

            new_visited = visited | (1 << index)

            next_dx = new_dx, next_dy = new_dy

            backtrack(nx, ny, new_dx, new_dy, new_visited, step_count +1)

        end for

    end for

Now, the problem reduces to implementing this with symmetry reduction. 

To remove symmetries, perhaps we can enforce that the path starts at (0,0) and the first move is the smallest possible in some ordering, thus representing one per equivalence class. 

Thus, in the first step (the first real step beyond start),

the first move must be to the smallest lex coordinate possible. 

For example, the first move must choose the first (lex smallest) unvisited point. 

This would enforce that for all paths in the equivalence classes, their first move must be the same, thus preventing considering multiple representatives. 

Thereby, this approach counts the number of equivalence classes under the symmetry group. 

Thus in the code, when making the first move ( step count = 2, since the first NON-starting move is ), we can manipulate the loop to 仅 generate the smallest possible first move.

修改 the recursive function to enfore that the first move is to the lex smallest possible point.

 这可以 be done by taking the next points in lex order and process the first one eligible, omitting all others. 

这样, we cannot branch, thus这一 approach can only find one path per equivalence class, equivalent to one canonical form. 

Thus, if the code just proceeds in that manner, 后 counting all such possible 真的 paths that satisfy the conditions and are the first in lex ordering, the total number is the desired count. 

 这 will reduce the search space dramatically but requires a way to ensure that only the canonical path is counted. 

To do this, we can enforce that, at each step, the next moved-to point is the smallest possible (lex) point that is both unvisited and satisfies the non-collinear condition. 

This can be done by iterating over all candidates in lex order, and branching only on the first eligible candidate. 

这样, the code will explores only one path per equivalence class (assuming symmetry rotations can be mapped to this path). 

The only way this works is if the first move is forced to be the lex smallest allowed one, which fixes the symmetry. 

Alternatively, maybe some equivalence classes have members with no lex smallest start.

Perhaps the best way is to implement the backtracking with symmetry breaking as follows:

在 each step, 生成的 next move is the smallest lexicographical point that satisfies the constraints. 这 way, the only one path per equivalent class is generated, thus the count is the number of equivalence classes.

例如，如果 the smallest path cannot complete the polygon, but some symmetrically equivalent path can.

Therefore, this approach may undercount or overcount.

import torch
import torch.nn as nn


class Loss(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, device, type_in='pred_intervals', alpha=0.1,
        loss_type='qd_soft', censor_R=False, soften=100.0, lambda_in=10.0,
        sigma_in=0.5):
        super().__init__()
        self.alpha = alpha
        self.lambda_in = lambda_in
        self.soften = soften
        self.loss_type = loss_type
        self.type_in = type_in
        self.censor_R = censor_R
        self.sigma_in = sigma_in
        self.device = device

    def forward(self, y_pred, y_true):
        if self.type_in == 'pred_intervals':
            metric = []
            metric_name = []
            y_U = y_pred[:, 0]
            y_L = y_pred[:, 1]
            y_T = y_true[:, 0]
            N_ = y_T.shape[0]
            alpha_ = self.alpha
            lambda_ = self.lambda_in
            torch.mean(y_pred, dim=1)
            MPIW = torch.mean(y_U - y_L)
            gamma_U = torch.sigmoid((y_U - y_T) * self.soften)
            gamma_L = torch.sigmoid((y_T - y_L) * self.soften)
            gamma_ = torch.mul(gamma_U, gamma_L)
            torch.ones_like(gamma_)
            zeros = torch.zeros_like(y_U)
            gamma_U_hard = torch.max(zeros, torch.sign(y_U - y_T))
            gamma_L_hard = torch.max(zeros, torch.sign(y_T - y_L))
            gamma_hard = torch.mul(gamma_U_hard, gamma_L_hard)
            qd_lhs_hard = torch.div(torch.mean(torch.abs(y_U - y_L) *
                gamma_hard), torch.mean(gamma_hard) + 0.001)
            torch.div(torch.mean(torch.abs(y_U - y_L) * gamma_), torch.mean
                (gamma_) + 0.001)
            PICP_soft = torch.mean(gamma_)
            PICP_hard = torch.mean(gamma_hard)
            zero = torch.tensor(0.0)
            qd_rhs_soft = lambda_ * math.sqrt(N_) * torch.pow(torch.max(
                zero, 1.0 - alpha_ - PICP_soft), 2)
            qd_rhs_hard = lambda_ * math.sqrt(N_) * torch.pow(torch.max(
                zero, 1.0 - alpha_ - PICP_hard), 2)
            qd_loss_soft = qd_lhs_hard + qd_rhs_soft
            qd_loss_hard = qd_lhs_hard + qd_rhs_hard
            y_mean = y_U
            y_var_limited = torch.min(y_L, torch.tensor(10.0))
            y_var = torch.max(torch.log(1.0 + torch.exp(y_var_limited)),
                torch.tensor(1e-05))
            self.y_mean = y_mean
            self.y_var = y_var
            gauss_loss = torch.log(y_var) / 2.0 + torch.div(torch.pow(y_T -
                y_mean, 2), 2.0 * y_var)
            gauss_loss = torch.mean(gauss_loss)
            if self.loss_type == 'qd_soft':
                loss = qd_loss_soft
            elif self.loss_type == 'qd_hard':
                loss = qd_loss_hard
            elif self.loss_type == 'gauss_like':
                loss = gauss_loss
            elif self.loss_type == 'picp':
                loss = PICP_hard
            elif self.loss_type == 'mse':
                loss = torch.mean(torch.pow(y_U - y_T, 2))
            torch.mean(gamma_U_hard)
            torch.mean(gamma_L_hard)
            PICP = torch.mean(gamma_hard)
            metric.append(PICP)
            metric_name.append('PICP')
            metric.append(MPIW)
            metric_name.append('MPIW')
        return loss, PICP, MPIW


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'device': 0}]
<#sep#> we shifted t = x - pi, the equation became t² + cot²(t + pi) = 16pi². But we noticed that cot(t + pi) = cot t, so the equation is t² + cot²t = 16pi². Then t ranges from -4pi to 4pi. Therefore, the solutions for t are in (-4pi,4pi), which corresponds to x = t + pi in (-3pi,5pi).

The intervals we analyzed for t were 8 intervals of length pi each: (-4pi, -3pi), (-3pi, -2pi), ..., (3pi,4pi). Each of these intervals has two solutions, so 8 x 2 = 16 solutions. Therefore, there are 16 intersection points.

But wait, let me verify for one of the intervals where maybe the quadratic term is too large. For example, in the interval (4pi - approaching 4pi), t approaches 4pi from the left. So t is near 4pi. Then t² is near 16pi², cot²t is near +infty. So f(t) = t² + cot²t -16pi² ≈ 16pi² + infty -16pi² = +infty. So at t = 4pi - ε, f(t) is +infty. However, the allowed t is up to 4pi (exclusive). Similarly for t approaching -4pi from the right.

But in the interval (3pi,4pi), we already checked, so in each interval, the function behaves the same.

But wait, let's check whether in the outermost intervals, like (-4pi, -3pi) and (3pi,4pi), the quadratic term might dominate more. Let me take t approaching -4pi from the right. Then t is just above -4pi, so t = -4pi + ε, ε approaching 0+. Then t² = ( -4pi + ε )² ≈ 16pi² -8pi ε + ε². cot²t = cot²(-4pi + ε) = cot²(ε) ≈ 1/ε² as ε→0+. So f(t) ≈ (16pi² -8pi ε) + (1/ε²) -16pi² ≈ (1/ε²) -8pi ε. As ε approaches 0+, this is dominated by 1/ε², which tends to +infty. Therefore, near t = -4pi, f(t) approaches +infty. At the midpoint t = -7pi/2, as we saw earlier, f(t) is negative. So there's a zero crossing here. Similarly, the same applies to t approaching 3pi, 4pi, etc. Therefore, even in the outer intervals, two solutions each.

Therefore, with 8 intervals and 2 solutions each, total of 16 solutions.

Wait, but let me consider if the equation could have fewer solutions in some intervals. For example, perhaps in some intervals, the minimum of t² + cot²t is above 16pi², so no solutions. Let me check. For example, in the interval (0, pi), the minimum of t² + cot²t occurs at t where derivative is zero. Let's compute the derivative.

But maybe it's simpler to check if the minimal value of t² + cot²t is less than 16pi² in each interval.

The minimal value in each interval occurs at t where the derivative is zero. Let's compute f(t) = t² + cot²t. Then f'(t) = 2t + 2 cot t (-csc²t). Setting f'(t) = 0:

2t - 2 cot t csc²t = 0 => t = cot t csc²t.

This equation is complicated to solve analytically. Instead, maybe we can estimate the minimal value.

In the interval (npi, (n+1)pi), the minimal value of f(t) = t² + cot²t occurs at t where cot t is zero, which is at t = (n + 0.5)pi. At that point, cot t = 0, so f(t) = t². For example, in interval (0, pi), the minimum is at t = pi/2, f(t) = (pi/2)^2 ≈ 2.467. In the interval (pi, 2pi), minimum at 3pi/2, f(t) = (3pi/2)^2 ≈ 22.207. In interval (2pi, 3pi), minimum at 5pi/2, f(t)= (5pi/2)^2 ≈ 61.685. Similarly, in (3pi,4pi), minimum at 7pi/2, f(t)= (7pi/2)^2 ≈ 120.9. On the negative side, in interval (-pi,0), minimum at -pi/2, f(t)= (pi/2)^2 ≈ 2.467. In (-2pi, -pi), minimum at -3pi/2, f(t)= (3pi/2)^2 ≈22.207. In (-3pi, -2pi), minimum at -5pi/2, f(t)= (5pi/2)^2 ≈61.685. In (-4pi, -3pi), minimum at -7pi/2, f(t)= (7pi/2)^2 ≈120.9.

Therefore, the minimal value of f(t) = t² + cot²t in each interval increases as |t| increases. For example, in the interval (3pi,4pi), the minimal value is (7pi/2)^2 ≈ 120.9. The equation is f(t) = 16pi² ≈ 157.913. So (7pi/2)^2 ≈ (3.5pi)^2 = 12.25pi² ≈120.9, which is less than 16pi²≈157.91. Therefore, at t =7pi/2, f(t)=12.25pi², which is less than 16pi², so the function f(t) = t² + cot²t -16pi² is negative at t=7pi/2. Therefore, since f(t) approaches +infty at the endpoints of the interval (3pi,4pi) and is negative at the midpoint, there are two solutions in this interval.

Similarly, in the interval (4pi,5pi)... Wait, no, the circle equation is only defined up to x =5pi. Wait, the original variable x is in (-3pi,5pi), but when shifted, t = x - pi is in (-4pi,4pi). So the interval (3pi,4pi) in t corresponds to x in (4pi,5pi). At x=5pi, that's the edge of the circle.

In the interval (3pi,4pi) for t, which is x in (4pi,5pi), the minimal value is 12.25pi² <16pi², so there are two solutions here. Similarly, in the interval (-4pi, -3pi), minimal value is same (7pi/2)^2 ≈12.25pi² <16pi². So two solutions there.

Therefore, even in the outermost intervals, the minimal value is below 16pi², so two solutions per interval. Therefore, 8 intervals x 2 solutions = 16 solutions. Therefore, the number of intersection points is 16.

But wait, let me confirm if there's any overlap or if any solutions are missed. Also, note that the equation is symmetric in t and x, but I don't think that affects the count. Alternatively, let's think about the graph.

The circle is quite large, radius 4pi, centered at (pi,0). The cotangent curve has asymptotes at multiples of pi. The circle will intersect each branch of the cotangent curve twice. Since between each asymptote, there's a branch of cotangent going from +infty to -infty (for positive x, in (0,pi) cot x goes from +infty to -infty, etc.), the circle might intersect each branch twice. Since there are 8 intervals (from -3pi to 5pi, which is 8pi range, divided into 8 intervals of pi each) each with two intersections, that gives 16 points.

But another way: from x = -3pi to x=5pi, there are 8 intervals between the asymptotes. In each interval, the circle intersects the cotangent curve twice, so 8*2=16.

Thus, the answer should be 16.

**Final Answer**
The number of intersection points is \boxed{16}.

def backtrack(x,y, prev_dx, prev_dy, visited, step_count, goals):

    if step_count == goals:

        # check to close loop.

        dx = 0-x, dy =0-y.

        命名 cross = prev_dx * dy - prev_dy * dx.

        if cross !=0:

            return 1.

        else:

            return0 

    count =0 

    # collect all possible next points.

    next_points = []

        for ny in 0到 n-1:

            index = nx n + ny 

            if not (visited & (1<< index)) : 

                next_points.append( (nx, ny) )

    # sort next_points by lex order (smallest first).

    next_points = sorted(next_points)

    # iterate through these, and if first valid one, proceed.

        new_dy = ny -y

import torch.nn as nn


class ActNorm(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, num_channels, scale=1.0, logscale_factor=3.0,
        batch_variance=False):
        """
        Activation normalization layer

        :param num_channels: number of channels
        :type num_channels: int
        :param scale: scale
        :type scale: float
        :param logscale_factor: factor for logscale
        :type logscale_factor: float
        :param batch_variance: use batch variance
        :type batch_variance: bool
        """
        super().__init__()
        self.num_channels = num_channels
        self.scale = scale
        self.logscale_factor = logscale_factor
        self.batch_variance = batch_variance
        self.bias_inited = False
        self.logs_inited = False
        self.register_parameter('bias', nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(1, self.
            num_channels, 1, 1)))
        self.register_parameter('logs', nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(1, self.
            num_channels, 1, 1)))

    def actnorm_center(self, x, reverse=False):
        """
        center operation of activation normalization

        :param x: input
        :type x: torch.Tensor
        :param reverse: whether to reverse bias
        :type reverse: bool
        :return: centered input
        :rtype: torch.Tensor
        """
        if not self.bias_inited:
            self.initialize_bias(x)
        if not reverse:
            return x + self.bias
        else:
            return x - self.bias

    def actnorm_scale(self, x, logdet, reverse=False):
        """
        scale operation of activation normalization

        :param x: input
        :type x: torch.Tensor
        :param logdet: log determinant
        :type logdet:
        :param reverse: whether to reverse bias
        :type reverse: bool
        :return: centered input and logdet
        :rtype: tuple(torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor)
        """
        if not self.logs_inited:
            self.initialize_logs(x)
        logs = self.logs * self.logscale_factor
        if not reverse:
            x *= torch.exp(logs)
        else:
            x *= torch.exp(-logs)
        if logdet is not None:
            logdet_factor = ops.count_pixels(x)
            dlogdet = torch.sum(logs) * logdet_factor
            if reverse:
                dlogdet *= -1
            logdet += dlogdet
        return x, logdet

    def initialize_bias(self, x):
        """
        Initialize bias

        :param x: input
        :type x: torch.Tensor
        """
        if not self.training:
            return
        with torch.no_grad():
            x_mean = -1.0 * ops.reduce_mean(x, dim=[0, 2, 3], keepdim=True)
            self.bias.data.copy_(x_mean.data)
            self.bias_inited = True

    def initialize_logs(self, x):
        """
        Initialize logs

        :param x: input
        :type x: torch.Tensor
        """
        if not self.training:
            return
        with torch.no_grad():
            if self.batch_variance:
                x_var = ops.reduce_mean(x ** 2, keepdim=True)
            else:
                x_var = ops.reduce_mean(x ** 2, dim=[0, 2, 3], keepdim=True)
            logs = torch.log(self.scale / (torch.sqrt(x_var) + 1e-06)
                ) / self.logscale_factor
            self.logs.data.copy_(logs.data)
            self.logs_inited = True

    def forward(self, x, logdet=None, reverse=False):
        """
        Forward activation normalization layer

        :param x: input
        :type x: torch.Tensor
        :param logdet: log determinant
        :type logdet:
        :param reverse: whether to reverse bias
        :type reverse: bool
        :return: normalized input and logdet
        :rtype: tuple(torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor)
        """
        assert len(x.shape) == 4
        assert x.shape[1
            ] == self.num_channels, 'Input shape should be NxCxHxW, however channels are {} instead of {}'.format(
            x.shape[1], self.num_channels)
        assert x.device == self.bias.device and x.device == self.logs.device, 'Expect input device {} instead of {}'.format(
            self.bias.device, x.device)
        if not reverse:
            x = self.actnorm_center(x, reverse=False)
            x, logdet = self.actnorm_scale(x, logdet, reverse=False)
        else:
            x, logdet = self.actnorm_scale(x, logdet, reverse=True)
            x = self.actnorm_center(x, reverse=True)
        return x, logdet


class Conv2d(nn.Conv2d):

    @staticmethod
    def get_padding(padding_type, kernel_size, stride):
        """
        Get padding size.

        mentioned in https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/issues/3867#issuecomment-361775080
        behaves as 'SAME' padding in TensorFlow
        independent on input size when stride is 1

        :param padding_type: type of padding in ['SAME', 'VALID']
        :type padding_type: str
        :param kernel_size: kernel size
        :type kernel_size: tuple(int) or int
        :param stride: stride
        :type stride: int
        :return: padding size
        :rtype: tuple(int)
        """
        assert padding_type in ['SAME', 'VALID'
            ], 'Unsupported padding type: {}'.format(padding_type)
        if isinstance(kernel_size, int):
            kernel_size = [kernel_size, kernel_size]
        if padding_type == 'SAME':
            assert stride == 1, "'SAME' padding only supports stride=1"
            return tuple((k - 1) // 2 for k in kernel_size)
        return tuple(0 for _ in kernel_size)

    def __init__(self, in_channels, out_channels, kernel_size=(3, 3),
        stride=1, padding_type='SAME', do_weightnorm=False, do_actnorm=True,
        dilation=1, groups=1):
        """
        Wrapper of nn.Conv2d with weight normalization and activation normalization

        :param padding_type: type of padding in ['SAME', 'VALID']
        :type padding_type: str
        :param do_weightnorm: whether to do weight normalization after convolution
        :type do_weightnorm: bool
        :param do_actnorm: whether to do activation normalization after convolution
        :type do_actnorm: bool
        """
        padding = self.get_padding(padding_type, kernel_size, stride)
        super().__init__(in_channels, out_channels, kernel_size, stride,
            padding, dilation, groups, bias=not do_actnorm)
        self.do_weight_norm = do_weightnorm
        self.do_actnorm = do_actnorm
        self.weight.data.normal_(mean=0.0, std=0.05)
        if self.do_actnorm:
            self.actnorm = ActNorm(out_channels)
        else:
            self.bias.data.zero_()

    def forward(self, x):
        """
        Forward wrapped Conv2d layer

        :param x: input
        :type x: torch.Tensor
        :return: output
        :rtype: torch.Tensor
        """
        x = super().forward(x)
        if self.do_actnorm:
            x, _ = self.actnorm(x)
        return x


class Conv2dZeros(nn.Conv2d):

    def __init__(self, in_channels, out_channels, kernel_size=(3, 3),
        stride=1, padding_type='SAME', logscale_factor=3, dilation=1,
        groups=1, bias=True):
        """
        Wrapper of nn.Conv2d with zero initialization and logs

        :param padding_type: type of padding in ['SAME', 'VALID']
        :type padding_type: str
        :param logscale_factor: factor for logscale
        :type logscale_factor: float
        """
        padding = Conv2d.get_padding(padding_type, kernel_size, stride)
        super().__init__(in_channels, out_channels, kernel_size, stride,
            padding, dilation, groups, bias)
        self.logscale_factor = logscale_factor
        self.bias.data.zero_()
        self.weight.data.zero_()
        self.register_parameter('logs', nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(
            out_channels, 1, 1)))

    def forward(self, x):
        """
        Forward wrapped Conv2d layer

        :param x: input
        :type x: torch.Tensor
        :return: output
        :rtype: torch.Tensor
        """
        x = super().forward(x)
        x *= torch.exp(self.logs * self.logscale_factor)
        return x

But another way: from x = -3pi to x=5pi, there are 8 intervals between the asymptotes. In each interval, the circle intersects the cotangent curve twice, so 8*2=16.

Thus, the answer should be 16.

**Final Answer**
The number of intersection points is \boxed{16}.
</think>To find the number of points where the curve \( y = \cot x \) intersects the circle given by \( x^2 + y^2 - 2\pi x - 15\pi^2 = 0 \), we start by rewriting the circle's equation in standard form. Completing the square for the \( x \)-terms, we get:

\[
(x - \pi)^2 + y^2 = 16\pi^2
\]

This represents a circle centered at \( (\pi, 0) \) with radius \( 4\pi \).

Substituting \( y = \cot x \) into the circle's equation, we obtain:

\[
(x - \pi)^2 + \cot^2 x = 16\pi^2
\]

To simplify the analysis, we shift the variable \( x \) to \( t = x - \pi \). Thus, the equation becomes:

\[
t^2 + \cot^2 t = 16\pi^2
\]

We need to solve this equation for \( t \) in the interval \( (-4\pi, 4\pi) \), excluding points where \( t \) is a multiple of \( \pi \) (since \( \cot t \) is undefined there). The interval \( (-4\pi, 4\pi) \) is divided into 8 intervals of length \( \pi \):

1. \( (-4\pi, -3\pi) \)
2. \( (-3\pi, -2\pi) \)
3. \( (-2\pi, -\pi) \)
4. \( (-\pi, 0) \)
5. \( (0, \pi) \)
6. \( (\pi, 2\pi) \)
7. \( (2\pi, 3\pi) \)
8. \( (3\pi, 4\pi) \)

In each interval, the function \( f(t) = t^2 + \cot^2 t - 16\pi^2 \) is analyzed. The term \( \cot^2 t \) starts at \( +\infty \) as \( t \) approaches the left endpoint of the interval, decreases to 0 at the midpoint \( t = \frac{(n+0.5)\pi} \), and then increases back to \( +\infty \) as \( t \) approaches the right endpoint of the interval. The quadratic term \( t^2 \) varies quadratically.

By evaluating the behavior of \( f(t) \) at the boundaries and midpoints of each interval, we find that \( f(t) \) crosses zero twice in each interval due to the Intermediate Value Theorem. Specifically:

- At \( t \) approaching the left endpoint, \( t^2 \) is small and \( \cot^2 t \) is large, making \( f(t) \) positive.
- At \( t = \frac{(n+0.5)\pi} \), \( \cot^2 t = 0 \), and \( f(t) \) is negative because \( t^2 < 16\pi^2 \).
- At \( t \) approaching the right endpoint, \( t^2 \) is large and \( \cot^2 t \) is large, making \( f(t) \) positive.

Thus, in each interval, \( f(t) \) crosses zero twice, indicating two solutions per interval.

Since there are 8 intervals, the total number of intersection points is:

\[
8 \times 2 = 16
\]

Therefore, the number of points where the curves intersect is \(\boxed{16}\).<#sep#> symmetry reduction to start at (0,0) and first move the minimal.

<#sep#>Provide an example of an endomorphism $\alpha$ of $\mathbb{R}^{3}$ that has nullity $2$ and is not diagonalizable.

<think>
Okay, so I need to find an example of an endomorphism (which is just a linear transformation from a vector space to itself) of ℝ³ that has nullity 2 and isn't diagonalizable. Hmm. Let me start by recalling some concepts. 

First, nullity is the dimension of the kernel of the linear transformation, right? So if the nullity is 2, that means the kernel has dimension 2. For a linear transformation represented by a matrix, the kernel is the set of all vectors that get mapped to the zero vector. The rank-nullity theorem says that rank plus nullity equals the dimension of the domain. Here, the domain is ℝ³, so rank + 2 = 3, which means the rank is 1. So the image of this transformation is one-dimensional.

Now, the transformation isn't diagonalizable. For a matrix to be diagonalizable, it needs to have a basis of eigenvectors. If it's not diagonalizable, that means it doesn't have enough eigenvectors; maybe it's defective. A common example of a non-diagonalizable matrix is a Jordan block, especially a 3x3 Jordan block with an eigenvalue of algebraic multiplicity 3 but geometric multiplicity 1. But wait, in this case, the nullity is 2, so the kernel is 2-dimensional. The kernel corresponds to the eigenvectors with eigenvalue 0. So if the nullity is 2, the geometric multiplicity of eigenvalue 0 is 2. But for a 3x3 matrix, the algebraic multiplicity has to be at least the geometric multiplicity, so the algebraic multiplicity of 0 would be at least 2. However, since the rank is 1, the rank is 3 - nullity = 1, which is also the dimension of the image. 

Wait, but if the nullity is 2, then the geometric multiplicity of eigenvalue 0 is 2. If the transformation isn't diagonalizable, then there must be at least one eigenvalue whose algebraic multiplicity is greater than its geometric multiplicity. So maybe 0 is an eigenvalue with algebraic multiplicity 3, but geometric multiplicity 2. But that's impossible because the geometric multiplicity can't exceed the algebraic multiplicity. Wait, no. Let me think again.

The algebraic multiplicity is the number of times the eigenvalue appears as a root of the characteristic polynomial. The geometric multiplicity is the dimension of the eigenspace. So if we have a matrix with eigenvalue 0, algebraic multiplicity 3, but geometric multiplicity 2, that would mean that in the Jordan form, there are two Jordan blocks for eigenvalue 0. Since the geometric multiplicity is the number of Jordan blocks, but the size of the blocks would be... Let's see, if algebraic multiplicity is 3, then the sum of the sizes of the Jordan blocks for 0 is 3. If there are two blocks, one of size 2 and one of size 1. But then the Jordan form would have two blocks: a 2x2 Jordan block and a 1x1 block. Wait, but the 2x2 Jordan block would have geometric multiplicity 1 for that block, but combined with the 1x1 block, the total geometric multiplicity would be 2. So that would work.

But then, if the Jordan form is a direct sum of a 2x2 Jordan block and a 1x1 block, all with eigenvalue 0, then the matrix would be similar to that Jordan matrix. However, such a matrix would have nullity 2. But wait, let's check. The Jordan matrix would look like:

[0 1 0]
[0 0 0]
[0 0 0]

Because the 2x2 Jordan block is [[0,1],[0,0]] and then a 0. Then, the rank of this matrix is 1 (since there's one non-zero row in the 2x2 block), so the nullity is 2. So that works. Also, this matrix is not diagonalizable because the Jordan block is not diagonal. So this would be an example. So maybe this is the example we need.

But in the problem, they want an endomorphism of ℝ³, so we can take this Jordan matrix as the matrix representation of α. So α would be represented by the matrix:

0 1 0

0 0 0

0 0 0

But let me verify. The nullity is 2. Let's compute the kernel. The kernel is the set of vectors (x, y, z) such that:

0x + 1y + 0z = 0

0x + 0y + 0z = 0

0x + 0y + 0z = 0

So the first equation gives y = 0, and the other two equations are 0=0. So the kernel is all vectors (x, 0, z), which is a 2-dimensional subspace. So nullity is indeed 2. The rank is 1, as there's only one pivot in the matrix. And this matrix is not diagonalizable because it's a Jordan block with a 1 in the superdiagonal. So if we try to diagonalize it, we can't find a basis of eigenvectors. The eigenvectors here would be only the vectors in the kernel, but the eigenspace is 2-dimensional, and algebraic multiplicity is 3, so it's deficient by one eigenvector. Therefore, the matrix isn't diagonalizable. So this seems to fit.

So then, the example is the linear transformation represented by the matrix with a 1 in the (1,2) entry and zeros elsewhere except the diagonal (but since it's Jordan, the diagonal entries are 0). 

Wait, but let me check again. The matrix I described:

[0 1 0]

[0 0 0]

[0 0 0]

Multiplying this matrix by a vector (x, y, z) gives (y, 0, 0). So the image is all vectors of the form (a, 0, 0), which is one-dimensional, so rank 1. The kernel is vectors with y=0, so (x, 0, z), which is two-dimensional. So nullity 2. That works. And the matrix is in Jordan form, so it's not diagonalizable. So this should be an example.

Alternatively, maybe there's another way to construct such a linear transformation. Maybe using a nilpotent matrix? A nilpotent matrix of index 2. Because if the matrix squared is zero, but the matrix itself is not zero, then it's nilpotent of index 2. For a 3x3 matrix, if it's nilpotent with index 2, then the Jordan form would have blocks of size 2 and 1. So the same as the matrix above. So this is a valid example.

Therefore, the answer is the linear transformation represented by the matrix:

0 1 0

0 0 0

0 0 0

But let me check if there's another possible matrix. For example, if we take a matrix with two Jordan blocks, one of size 2 and one of size 1, but with different eigenvalues. Wait, but the problem just says "endomorphism of ℝ³", so eigenvalues can be anything. However, if we take the eigenvalue 0 with algebraic multiplicity 3 and geometric multiplicity 2, that gives us the example above. If we use another eigenvalue, say 1, but then the nullity would be different. Wait, the nullity is the dimension of the kernel, which corresponds to the geometric multiplicity of eigenvalue 0. So if the transformation has other eigenvalues, the kernel could still be related to eigenvalue 0. So maybe another example could have eigenvalues 0 and something else, but the nullity is 2. Hmm. Let's see.

Suppose we have a matrix with eigenvalues 0, 0, λ where λ ≠ 0. But to have nullity 2, the geometric multiplicity of 0 must be 2. So the Jordan blocks for 0 would be two 1x1 blocks and one 1x1 block? Wait, no. Wait, if the eigenvalue 0 has algebraic multiplicity 2 and geometric multiplicity 2, then it's diagonalizable. So the Jordan form would be diag(0,0,λ). But then the matrix is diagonalizable, so that's not what we want. The problem states the endomorphism is not diagonalizable, so we can't have that.

Therefore,<#sep#>human: Introduction
A known fact is that Jon Skeet can code Perl and make it look like Java. In this challenge you will try to do something similar.
Challenge
Write a piece of code of any length that can be executed without any errors/exceptions in two different languages. Your program can take inputs, give outputs, whatever it wants.
The code has to be executed with interpreters/compilers of your choice one after another (with basic settings, means you are not allowed to adjust the compiler/interpreter settings).
Note that the source code and any used sources (files, libs and so on) are not allowed to change during the execution of the script with the compilers/interpreters.
But which languages are "different"?
Two languages are different from each other if they are not the same language without looking at the version. Example: Python 3.5 is not different to Python 2.7.
Scoring
The final score consists out of several aspects:

charScore: Take your code and then (max charScore is 95)...

Look at every function and delete all arguments (in echo "Hello" "Hello" counts as an argument). So now only keywords and functions count. Example: System.out.println(1+1); becomes System.out.println();
Make all strings to empty strings and all regex and literals empty. Example: "khffshiufahk" becomes "" and all regex (without string) gets deleted.
Delete all comments. Example: //Jon Skeet would also do this becomes [nothing]
_Definition of "comment": _ Anything that doesn't change the execution of the program (So you could simply leave them out and nothing would change).
Delete all characters that are not unicode 32-126. Example: print(2²) becomes print(2)
Count only the occurrences of the letters. Example: hiiiiiii-thhheeeree has the same score as hi-there (means score of 8)

Here is a StackSnippet to do step 3 and 4 for you (report me if there is a bug):


function calcScore() {
 var onlyValidChars = "";
 var code = document.getElementById("code").value;
 for (i = 0; i < code.length; i++) {
  if (code[i].charCodeAt(0)>=32 && code[i].charCodeAt(0)<=126) { //step 3
     if (onlyValidChars.indexOf(code[i]) === -1) { //step 4
       onlyValidChars += code[i];
         }
       }
 }
  return onlyValidChars.length;
}
function showCalcScore() {
 alert("Your charScore: "+calcScore());
}

    <button type="button" onclick="showCalcScore();">calculate score</button>

from typing import Optional
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.parallel
import torch.optim
import torch.utils.data
import torch.utils.data.distributed


class GaussianKernel(nn.Module):
    """Gaussian Kernel Matrix

    Gaussian Kernel k is defined by

    .. math::
        k(x_1, x_2) = \\exp \\left( - \\dfrac{\\| x_1 - x_2 \\|^2}{2\\sigma^2} \\right)

    where :math:`x_1, x_2 \\in R^d` are 1-d tensors.

    Gaussian Kernel Matrix K is defined on input group :math:`X=(x_1, x_2, ..., x_m),`

    .. math::
        K(X)_{i,j} = k(x_i, x_j)

    Also by default, during training this layer keeps running estimates of the
    mean of L2 distances, which are then used to set hyperparameter  :math:`\\sigma`.
    Mathematically, the estimation is :math:`\\sigma^2 = \\dfrac{\\alpha}{n^2}\\sum_{i,j} \\| x_i - x_j \\|^2`.
    If :attr:`track_running_stats` is set to ``False``, this layer then does not
    keep running estimates, and use a fixed :math:`\\sigma` instead.

    Parameters:
        - sigma (float, optional): bandwidth :math:`\\sigma`. Default: None
        - track_running_stats (bool, optional): If ``True``, this module tracks the running mean of :math:`\\sigma^2`.
          Otherwise, it won't track such statistics and always uses fix :math:`\\sigma^2`. Default: ``True``
        - alpha (float, optional): :math:`\\alpha` which decides the magnitude of :math:`\\sigma^2` when track_running_stats is set to ``True``

    Inputs:
        - X (tensor): input group :math:`X`

    Shape:
        - Inputs: :math:`(minibatch, F)` where F means the dimension of input features.
        - Outputs: :math:`(minibatch, minibatch)`
    """

    def __init__(self, sigma: 'Optional[float]'=None, track_running_stats:
        'Optional[bool]'=True, alpha: 'Optional[float]'=1.0):
        super(GaussianKernel, self).__init__()
        assert track_running_stats or sigma is not None
        self.sigma_square = torch.tensor(sigma * sigma
            ) if sigma is not None else None
        self.track_running_stats = track_running_stats
        self.alpha = alpha

    def forward(self, X: 'torch.Tensor') ->torch.Tensor:
        l2_distance_square = ((X.unsqueeze(0) - X.unsqueeze(1)) ** 2).sum(2)
        if self.track_running_stats:
            self.sigma_square = self.alpha * torch.mean(l2_distance_square.
                detach())
        return torch.exp(-l2_distance_square / (2 * self.sigma_square))


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]

Phase 7. VERIFICATION:<#sep#>import torch
import numpy as np
from torch import nn
from torch.nn.functional import relu


def regula_falsi(func, a, b, iterations):
    f_a = func(a, -1)
    f_b = func(b, -1)
    if torch.any(f_a * f_b >= 0):
        None
        raise Exception(
            'You have not assumed right initial values in regula falsi')
    c = a
    break_indices = torch.zeros_like(a).bool()
    for i in range(iterations):
        c = (a * f_b - b * f_a) / (f_b - f_a)
        f_c = func(c, i)
        break_indices[f_c == 0] = True
        b_eq_c_indices = (f_c * f_a < 0) & ~break_indices
        b[b_eq_c_indices] = c[b_eq_c_indices]
        a_eq_c_indices = ~(b_eq_c_indices | break_indices)
        a[a_eq_c_indices] = c[a_eq_c_indices]
    return c


class relu_constant_fraction(nn.Module):

    def __init__(self, nb_channels):
        super(relu_constant_fraction, self).__init__()
        self.biases = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(nb_channels))
        self.biases.requires_grad = False
        self.bias_buffer = None

    def forward(self, x):
        return relu(x - self.biases.view(1, -1, 1, 1))

    def adjust_bias(self, desired_fraction, prev_layer_outputs):
        if desired_fraction > 1 - 0.001:
            self.biases.data = -10 * torch.ones_like(self.biases)
            return

        def get_fraction_deviation(biases, j):
            activations = relu(prev_layer_outputs - biases.view(1, -1, 1, 1))
            ratios = (activations > 0.001).float().mean(dim=(0, 2, 3))
            return ratios - desired_fraction
        with torch.no_grad():
            solutions = regula_falsi(get_fraction_deviation, -3 * torch.
                ones_like(self.biases), 3 * torch.ones_like(self.biases), 20)
            momentum = 0.75
            dampening = 0.0
            lr = 0.5
            delta = solutions - self.biases
            buf = self.bias_buffer
            if buf is None:
                buf = torch.clone(delta).detach()
                self.bias_buffer = buf
            else:
                buf.mul_(momentum).add_(delta, alpha=1 - dampening)
            delta = buf
            self.biases.add_(delta, alpha=lr)

    def get_activation_fractions(self, prev_layer_outputs):
        activations = relu(prev_layer_outputs - self.biases.view(1, -1, 1, 1))
        ratios = (activations > 0.001).float().mean(dim=(0, 2, 3))
        return ratios

    def show_trajectory(self, prev_layer_outputs):
        import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
        bias_values = np.linspace(-10, 10, 1000)
        fractions = np.zeros((1000, self.biases.shape[0]))
        for j, bias in enumerate(bias_values):
            cumulative_ratios = torch.zeros_like(self.biases)
            batch_size = 1000
            for i in range(0, len(prev_layer_outputs), batch_size):
                data = prev_layer_outputs[i:i + batch_size]
                activations = relu(data - bias)
                cumulative_ratios += (activations > 0.001).float().mean(dim
                    =(0, 2, 3)) * len(data)
            fractions[j] = (cumulative_ratios / len(prev_layer_outputs)
                ).detach().cpu().numpy()
        plt.plot(bias_values, fractions)
        plt.show()


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'nb_channels': 4}]

</think>To find an endomorphism \(\alpha\) of \(\mathbb{R}^3\) that has nullity 2 and is not diagonalizable, we need to construct a matrix with the following properties:
1. The nullity of the matrix should be 2, meaning the dimension of the kernel is 2.
2. The matrix should not be diagonalizable, implying it has a Jordan block structure.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Determine the Rank and Nullity:**
   - The nullity of the matrix is 2, so the dimension of the kernel is 2.
   - By the rank-nullity theorem, the rank of the matrix is \(3 - 2 = 1\).

2. **Eigenvalues and Eigenspaces:**
   - Since the nullity is 2, the eigenvalue 0 must have a geometric multiplicity of 2.
   - For the matrix to be non-diagonalizable, the algebraic multiplicity of the eigenvalue 0 must be 3, and there must be a Jordan block of size 2.

3. **Construct the Matrix:**
   - A matrix with a Jordan block of size 2 for the eigenvalue 0 and a 1x1 block for the eigenvalue 0 will have the form:
     \[
     \begin{pmatrix}
     0 & 1 & 0 \\
     0 & 0 & 0 \\
     0 & 0 & 0
     \end{pmatrix}
     \]

4. **Verify Nullity:**
   - The kernel of the matrix is the set of vectors \((x, y, z)\) such that:
     \[
     \begin{pmatrix}
     0 & 1 & 0 \\
     0 & 0 & 0 \\
     0 & 0 & 0
     \end{pmatrix}
     \begin{pmatrix}
     x \\
     y \\
     z
     \end{pmatrix}
     =
     \begin{pmatrix}
     y \\
     0 \\
     0
     \end{pmatrix}
     = \begin{pmatrix}
     0 \\
     0 \\
     0
     \end{pmatrix}
     \]
   - This implies \(y = 0\). Thus, the kernel is spanned by \((1, 0, 0)\) and \((0, 0, 1)\), which is 2-dimensional. Therefore, the nullity is 2.

5. **Verify Rank:**
   - The rank of the matrix is the number of non-zero rows in its row echelon form, which is 1 (the first row is non-zero).

6. **Non-Diagonalizability:**
   - The matrix is not diagonalizable because it has a Jordan block of size 2. The Jordan form of the matrix is:
     \[
     \begin{pmatrix}
     0 & 1 & 0 \\
     0 & 0 & 0 \\
     0 & 0 & 0
     \end{pmatrix}
     \]
   - This form indicates that the matrix cannot be diagonalized to a diagonal matrix with only zeros on the diagonal.

Thus, the endomorphism \(\alpha\) can be represented by the matrix:
\[
\boxed{\begin{pmatrix}
0 & 1 & 0 \\
0 & 0 & 0 \\
0 & 0 & 0
\end{pmatrix}}

from typing import Optional
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F
import torch.nn.parallel
import torch.optim
import torch.utils.data
import torch.utils.data.distributed


def shift_log(x: 'torch.Tensor', offset: 'Optional[float]'=1e-06
    ) ->torch.Tensor:
    """
    First shift, then calculate log, which can be described as:

    .. math::
        y = \\max(\\log(x+\\text{offset}), 0)

    Used to avoid the gradient explosion problem in log(x) function when x=0.

    Parameters:
        - **x**: input tensor
        - **offset**: offset size. Default: 1e-6

    .. note::
        Input tensor falls in [0., 1.] and the output tensor falls in [-log(offset), 0]
    """
    return torch.log(torch.clamp(x + offset, max=1.0))


class MarginDisparityDiscrepancy(nn.Module):
    """The margin disparity discrepancy (MDD) is proposed to measure the distribution discrepancy in domain adaptation.

    The :math:`y^s` and :math:`y^t` are logits output by the main classifier on the source and target domain respectively.
    The :math:`y_{adv}^s` and :math:`y_{adv}^t` are logits output by the adversarial classifier.
    They are expected to contain raw, unnormalized scores for each class.

    The definition can be described as:

    .. math::
        \\mathcal{D}_{\\gamma}(\\hat{\\mathcal{S}}, \\hat{\\mathcal{T}}) =
        \\gamma \\mathbb{E}_{y^s, y_{adv}^s \\sim\\hat{\\mathcal{S}}} \\log\\left(\\frac{\\exp(y_{adv}^s[h_{y^s}])}{\\sum_j \\exp(y_{adv}^s[j])}\\right) +
        \\mathbb{E}_{y^t, y_{adv}^t \\sim\\hat{\\mathcal{T}}} \\log\\left(1-\\frac{\\exp(y_{adv}^t[h_{y^t}])}{\\sum_j \\exp(y_{adv}^t[j])}\\right),

    where :math:`\\gamma` is a margin hyper-parameter and :math:`h_y` refers to the predicted label when the logits output is :math:`y`.
    You can see more details in `Bridging Theory and Algorithm for Domain Adaptation <https://arxiv.org/abs/1904.05801>`_.

    Parameters:
        - **margin** (float): margin :math:`\\gamma`. Default: 4
        - **reduction** (string, optional): Specifies the reduction to apply to the output:
          ``'none'`` | ``'mean'`` | ``'sum'``. ``'none'``: no reduction will be applied,
          ``'mean'``: the sum of the output will be divided by the number of
          elements in the output, ``'sum'``: the output will be summed. Default: ``'mean'``

    Inputs: y_s, y_s_adv, y_t, y_t_adv
        - **y_s**: logits output :math:`y^s` by the main classifier on the source domain
        - **y_s_adv**: logits output :math:`y^s` by the adversarial classifier on the source domain
        - **y_t**: logits output :math:`y^t` by the main classifier on the target domain
        - **y_t_adv**: logits output :math:`y_{adv}^t` by the adversarial classifier on the target domain

    Shape:
        - Inputs: :math:`(minibatch, C)` where C = number of classes, or :math:`(minibatch, C, d_1, d_2, ..., d_K)`
          with :math:`K \\geq 1` in the case of `K`-dimensional loss.
        - Output: scalar. If :attr:`reduction` is ``'none'``, then the same size as the target: :math:`(minibatch)`, or
          :math:`(minibatch, d_1, d_2, ..., d_K)` with :math:`K \\geq 1` in the case of K-dimensional loss.

    Examples::
        >>> num_classes = 2
        >>> batch_size = 10
        >>> loss = MarginDisparityDiscrepancy(margin=4.)
        >>> # logits output from source domain and target domain
        >>> y_s, y_t = torch.randn(batch_size, num_classes), torch.randn(batch_size, num_classes)
        >>> # adversarial logits output from source domain and target domain
        >>> y_s_adv, y_t_adv = torch.randn(batch_size, num_classes), torch.randn(batch_size, num_classes)
        >>> output = loss(y_s, y_s_adv, y_t, y_t_adv)
    """

    def __init__(self, margin: 'Optional[int]'=4, reduction:
        'Optional[str]'='mean'):
        super(MarginDisparityDiscrepancy, self).__init__()
        self.margin = margin
        self.reduction = reduction

    def forward(self, y_s: 'torch.Tensor', y_s_adv: 'torch.Tensor', y_t:
        'torch.Tensor', y_t_adv: 'torch.Tensor') ->torch.Tensor:
        _, prediction_s = y_s.max(dim=1)
        _, prediction_t = y_t.max(dim=1)
        return self.margin * F.cross_entropy(y_s_adv, prediction_s,
            reduction=self.reduction) + F.nll_loss(shift_log(1.0 - F.
            softmax(y_t_adv, dim=1)), prediction_t, reduction=self.reduction)


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand(
        [4, 4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]

import torch
from torch import Tensor
from typing import Union
from typing import Tuple
import torch.nn.functional as F


class DivisiveNormalization2d(Module):
    """Applies a 2D divisive normalization over an input signal composed of several input
    planes.

    In the simplest case, the output value of the layer with input size :math:`(N, C, H, W)`
    and output :math:`(N, C, H, W)`.

    Args:
        b_type: Type of suppressin field, must be one of (`linf`, `l1`, `l2`).
        b_size: The size of the suppression field, must be > 0.
        sigma: Constant added to suppression field, must be > 0.

    Shape:
        - Input: :math:`(N, C, H, W)`
        - Output: :math:`(N, C, H, W)`

    Examples::

        >>> # suppression of size=3, sigma=1
        >>> d = DivisiveNormalization2d(b_size=3, sigma=1)
        >>> input = torch.randn(20, 16, 50, 50)
        >>> output = d(input)
    """

    def __init__(self, b_type: 'str'='linf', b_size:
        'Union[int, Tuple[int, int]]'=(5, 5), sigma: 'float'=1.0) ->None:
        super(DivisiveNormalization2d, self).__init__()
        self.sigma = sigma
        if isinstance(b_size, int):
            self.b_size = b_size, b_size
        else:
            self.b_size = b_size
        self.padding = self.b_size[0] // 2, self.b_size[1] // 2
        self.b_type = b_type

    def forward(self, input: 'Tensor') ->Tensor:
        if self.b_type == 'linf':
            suppression_field = F.max_pool2d(torch.abs(input), self.b_size,
                1, self.padding, 1)
        elif self.b_type == 'l1':
            weight = torch.ones((input.shape[1], 1, self.b_size[0], self.
                b_size[1]))
            suppression_field = F.conv2d(torch.abs(input), weight=weight,
                padding=self.padding, groups=input.shape[1])
        elif self.b_type == 'l2':
            weight = torch.ones((input.shape[1], 1, self.b_size[0], self.
                b_size[1]))
            suppression_field = torch.sqrt(F.conv2d(input ** 2, weight=
                weight, padding=self.padding, groups=input.shape[1]))
        else:
            raise NotImplementedError
        return input / (self.sigma + suppression_field)

    def __repr__(self) ->str:
        s = 'DivisiveNormalization2d('
        s += f'b_type={self.b_type}, b_size={self.b_size}, sigma={self.sigma}'
        s += ')'
        return s.format(**self.__dict__)


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]

================================================================<#sep#>import torch
import torch.nn as nn


class Attention(nn.Module):
    """ Applies attention mechanism on the `context` using the `query`.

    **Thank you** to IBM for their initial implementation of :class:`Attention`. Here is
    their `License
    <https://github.com/IBM/pytorch-seq2seq/blob/master/LICENSE>`__.

    Args:
        dimensions (int): Dimensionality of the query and context.
        attention_type (str, optional): How to compute the attention score:

            * dot: :math:`score(H_j,q) = H_j^T q`
            * general: :math:`score(H_j, q) = H_j^T W_a q`

    Example:

         >>> attention = Attention(256)
         >>> query = torch.randn(5, 1, 256)
         >>> context = torch.randn(5, 5, 256)
         >>> output, weights = attention(query, context)
         >>> output.size()
         torch.Size([5, 1, 256])
         >>> weights.size()
         torch.Size([5, 1, 5])
    """

    def __init__(self, dimensions, attention_type='general'):
        super(Attention, self).__init__()
        if attention_type not in ['dot', 'general']:
            raise ValueError('Invalid attention type selected.')
        self.attention_type = attention_type
        if self.attention_type == 'general':
            self.linear_in = nn.Linear(dimensions, dimensions, bias=False)
        self.linear_out = nn.Linear(dimensions * 2, dimensions, bias=False)
        self.softmax = nn.Softmax(dim=-1)
        self.tanh = nn.Tanh()

    def forward(self, query, context):
        """
        Args:
            query (:class:`torch.FloatTensor` [batch size, output length, dimensions]): Sequence of
                queries to query the context.
            context (:class:`torch.FloatTensor` [batch size, query length, dimensions]): Data
                overwhich to apply the attention mechanism.

        Returns:
            :class:`tuple` with `output` and `weights`:
            * **output** (:class:`torch.LongTensor` [batch size, output length, dimensions]):
              Tensor containing the attended features.
            * **weights** (:class:`torch.FloatTensor` [batch size, output length, query length]):
              Tensor containing attention weights.
        """
        batch_size, output_len, dimensions = query.size()
        query_len = context.size(1)
        if self.attention_type == 'general':
            query = query.view(batch_size * output_len, dimensions)
            query = self.linear_in(query)
            query = query.view(batch_size, output_len, dimensions)
        attention_scores = torch.bmm(query, context.transpose(1, 2).
            contiguous())
        attention_scores = attention_scores.view(batch_size * output_len,
            query_len)
        attention_weights = self.softmax(attention_scores)
        attention_weights = attention_weights.view(batch_size, output_len,
            query_len)
        mix = torch.bmm(attention_weights, context)
        combined = torch.cat((mix, query), dim=2)
        combined = combined.view(batch_size * output_len, 2 * dimensions)
        output = self.linear_out(combined).view(batch_size, output_len,
            dimensions)
        output = self.tanh(output)
        return output, attention_weights


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4]), torch.rand([4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'dimensions': 4}]

Multiply both sides by -1:

(n + C)^2 / (n + 1 + C ) = n + C - 1

Multiply both sides by (n + 1 + C ):

So,

(n + C)^2 = (n + C)^2 - 1

Which simplifies to 0 = -1, which is a contradiction. Therefore, this suggests that our initial assumption is incorrect, and the solution isn't of the form -1/(n + C). So the continuous approximation doesn't hold here. Therefore, maybe a different approach is needed.

## Phase 3. EXPLORATION

Let<#sep#>import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as fn
from torch.nn.parameter import Parameter
import torch.nn


def to_pair(data):
    """Converts a single or a tuple of data into a pair. If the data is a tuple with more than two elements, it selects
	the first two of them. In case of single data, it duplicates that data into a pair.

	Args:
		data (object or tuple): The input data.

	Returns:
		Tuple: A pair of data.
	"""
    if isinstance(data, tuple):
        return data[0:2]
    return data, data


class Convolution(nn.Module):
    """Performs a 2D convolution over an input spike-wave composed of several input
	planes. Current version only supports stride of 1 with no padding.

	The input is a 4D tensor with the size :math:`(T, C_{{in}}, H_{{in}}, W_{{in}})` and the crresponsing output
	is of size :math:`(T, C_{{out}}, H_{{out}}, W_{{out}})`, 
	where :math:`T` is the number of time steps, :math:`C` is the number of feature maps (channels), and
	:math:`H`, and :math:`W` are the hight and width of the input/output planes.

	* :attr:`in_channels` controls the number of input planes (channels/feature maps).

	* :attr:`out_channels` controls the number of feature maps in the current layer.

	* :attr:`kernel_size` controls the size of the convolution kernel. It can be a single integer or a tuple of two integers.

	* :attr:`weight_mean` controls the mean of the normal distribution used for initial random weights.

	* :attr:`weight_std` controls the standard deviation of the normal distribution used for initial random weights.

	.. note::

		Since this version of convolution does not support padding, it is the user responsibility to add proper padding
		on the input before applying convolution.

	Args:
		in_channels (int): Number of channels in the input.
		out_channels (int): Number of channels produced by the convolution.
		kernel_size (int or tuple): Size of the convolving kernel.
		weight_mean (float, optional): Mean of the initial random weights. Default: 0.8
		weight_std (float, optional): Standard deviation of the initial random weights. Default: 0.02
	"""

    def __init__(self, in_channels, out_channels, kernel_size, weight_mean=
        0.8, weight_std=0.02):
        super(Convolution, self).__init__()
        self.in_channels = in_channels
        self.out_channels = out_channels
        self.kernel_size = to_pair(kernel_size)
        self.stride = 1
        self.bias = None
        self.dilation = 1
        self.groups = 1
        self.padding = 0
        self.weight = Parameter(torch.Tensor(self.out_channels, self.
            in_channels, *self.kernel_size))
        self.weight.requires_grad_(False)
        self.reset_weight(weight_mean, weight_std)

    def reset_weight(self, weight_mean=0.8, weight_std=0.02):
        """Resets weights to random values based on a normal distribution.

		Args:
			weight_mean (float, optional): Mean of the random weights. Default: 0.8
			weight_std (float, optional): Standard deviation of the random weights. Default: 0.02
		"""
        self.weight.normal_(weight_mean, weight_std)

    def load_weight(self, target):
        """Loads weights with the target tensor.

		Args:
			target (Tensor=): The target tensor.
		"""
        self.weight.copy_(target)

    def forward(self, input):
        return fn.conv2d(input, self.weight, self.bias, self.stride, self.
            padding, self.dilation, self.groups)


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.rand([4, 4, 4, 4])]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {'in_channels': 4, 'out_channels': 4, 'kernel_size': 4}]
<#sep#> However, note that 1/d_{n} - 1/(d_n + 1) = 1/d_{n+1} So, if I sum from n=0 to n=k-1:

import numpy as np
from torch import nn
from typing import Optional
import torch.nn.parallel
import torch.optim
import torch.utils.data
import torch.utils.data.distributed


def _reduction(loss: 'torch.Tensor', reduction: 'str') ->torch.Tensor:
    """
    Reduce loss
    Parameters
    ----------
    loss : torch.Tensor, [batch_size, num_classes]
        Batch losses.
    reduction : str
        Method for reducing the loss. Options include 'elementwise_mean',
        'none', and 'sum'.
    Returns
    -------
    loss : torch.Tensor
        Reduced loss.
    """
    if reduction == 'elementwise_mean':
        return loss.mean()
    elif reduction == 'none':
        return loss
    elif reduction == 'sum':
        return loss.sum()
    else:
        raise ValueError(f'{reduction} is not a valid reduction')


def cumulative_link_loss(y_pred: 'torch.Tensor', y_true: 'torch.Tensor',
    reduction: 'str'='elementwise_mean', class_weights:
    'Optional[np.ndarray]'=None) ->torch.Tensor:
    """
    Calculates the negative log likelihood using the logistic cumulative link
    function.
    See "On the consistency of ordinal regression methods", Pedregosa et. al.
    for more details. While this paper is not the first to introduce this, it
    is the only one that I could find that was easily readable outside of
    paywalls.
    Parameters
    ----------
    y_pred : torch.Tensor, [batch_size, num_classes]
        Predicted target class probabilities. float dtype.
    y_true : torch.Tensor, [batch_size, 1]
        True target classes. long dtype.
    reduction : str
        Method for reducing the loss. Options include 'elementwise_mean',
        'none', and 'sum'.
    class_weights : np.ndarray, [num_classes] optional (default=None)
        An array of weights for each class. If included, then for each sample,
        look up the true class and multiply that sample's loss by the weight in
        this array.
    Returns
    -------
    loss: torch.Tensor
    """
    eps = 1e-15
    likelihoods = torch.clamp(torch.gather(y_pred, 1, y_true.unsqueeze(1)),
        eps, 1 - eps)
    neg_log_likelihood = -torch.log(likelihoods)
    if class_weights is not None:
        class_weights = torch.as_tensor(class_weights, dtype=
            neg_log_likelihood.dtype, device=neg_log_likelihood.device)
        neg_log_likelihood *= class_weights[y_true]
    loss = _reduction(neg_log_likelihood, reduction)
    return loss


class CumulativeLinkLoss(nn.Module):
    """
    Module form of cumulative_link_loss() loss function
    Parameters
    ----------
    reduction : str
        Method for reducing the loss. Options include 'elementwise_mean',
        'none', and 'sum'.
    class_weights : np.ndarray, [num_classes] optional (default=None)
        An array of weights for each class. If included, then for each sample,
        look up the true class and multiply that sample's loss by the weight in
        this array.
    """

    def __init__(self, reduction: 'str'='elementwise_mean', class_weights:
        'Optional[torch.Tensor]'=None) ->None:
        super().__init__()
        self.class_weights = class_weights
        self.reduction = reduction

    def forward(self, y_pred: 'torch.Tensor', y_true: 'torch.Tensor'
        ) ->torch.Tensor:
        return cumulative_link_loss(y_pred, y_true, reduction=self.
            reduction, class_weights=self.class_weights)


def get_inputs():
    return [torch.ones([4, 4], dtype=torch.int64), torch.ones([4], dtype=
        torch.int64)]


def get_init_inputs():
    return [[], {}]
<#sep#> that the recurrence is exactly solvable, but I need to confirm.

Earlier, we transformed the recurrence into d_{n+1} = d_n² + d_n, where d_n = b_n - 1/2 and b_n = a_n - (a - 1/2). Hmm, not sure.

Wait, let's retrace the substitutions:

Original recurrence: a_{n+1} = a_n² + (1 - 2a)a_n + a².

We set b_n = a_n - c, with c = a - 1/2, leading to b_{n+1} = b_n² + 1/4.

Then, we set d_n = b_n - 1/2, leading to d_{n+1} = d_n² + d_n.

Alternatively, perhaps we can get a closed-form solution by observing that the recurrence for d_n is d_{n+1} = d_n(1 + d_n). This is similar to the multiplicative recurrence which can be solved using reciprocals. Let me try again:

Let’s define e_n = 1/d_n. Then:

d_n = 1/e_n

So,

d_{n+1} = d_n(1 + d_n ) = (1/e_n)(1 + 1/e_n ) = (1 + e_n)/e_n^2

But d_{n+1} = 1/e_{n+1}, so:

1/e_{n+1} = (1 + e_n)/e_n^2

Therefore,

e_{n+1} = e_n^2 / (1 + e_n )

This recurrence still seems complicated, but perhaps we can telescope it. Let's take the reciprocal of both sides:

1/e_{n+1} = (1 + e_n)/e_n^2 = 1/e_n^2 + 1/e_n

Let’s write this as:

1/e_{n+1} = 1/e_n + 1/e_n^2

Hmm. If I let f_n = 1/e_n, then:

f_{n+1} = f_n + f_n^2

But this is exactly the same recurrence as d_n. So we’re back where we started. Not helpful.

Alternatively, suppose we iterate the recurrence for e_n:

e_{n+1} = e_n² / (1 + e_n )

This suggests:

e_{n+1} = e_n² / (1 + e_n )

Let’s compute the first few terms in terms of e_0:

e_1 = e_0² / (1 + e_0 )

e_2 = (e_0² / (1 + e_0 ))² / (1 + e_0² / (1 + e_0 )) )

= e_0^4 / ( (1 + e_0 )² * ( (1 + e_0 ) + e_0² ) / (1 + e_0 ) )

= e_0^4 / ( (1 + e_0 ) * (1 + e_0 + e_0² ) )

Hmm, getting messy. Maybe this approach isn't helpful.

Alternatively, since we transformed the original recurrence into d_{n+1} = d_n(1 + d_n ), we can write:

d_{n} = d_{n-1}(1 + d_{n-1}) = d_{n-1} + d_{n-1}^2

But if we expand this recursively:

d_{n} = d_0 + d_0^2 + d_0^3 + ... + d_0^{2^n}

Wait, no. Actually, each term is the product of previous terms. Let me see:

d_1 = d_0(1 + d_0 )

d_2 = d_1(1 + d_1 ) = d_0(1 + d_0 )(1 + d_0(1 + d_0 ) )

= d_0(1 + d_0 )(1 + d_0 + d_0² )

This is getting more complex. Perhaps there's a product formula. Let's think:

d_n = d_0 * (1 + d_0 ) * (1 + d_1 ) * ... * (1 + d_{n-1} )

Because:

d_{k+1} = d_k (1 + d_k )

So,

d_n = d_0 * Product_{k=0}^{n-1} (1 + d_k )

But this seems like an infinite product if we let n approach infinity, but without knowing more about d_k, it's hard to evaluate.

Alternatively, maybe take logarithms. Let’s take the logarithm of both sides:

ln(d_n ) = ln(d_0 ) + Sum_{k=0}^{n-1} ln(1 + d_k )

But this doesn’t obviously simplify the recurrence.

Given that I’m stuck with solving the recurrence for d_n, maybe I should think back to the original problem. The question is to determine the behavior or find a closed form for the sequence {a_n}. Perhaps instead of trying to find an explicit formula, we can analyze the behavior based on the initial term.

Let’s recall that when we transformed the original recurrence, we ended up with b_{n+1} = b_n² + 1/4, and we made the substitution b_n = a_n - (a - 1/2). The fixed point in terms of b_n is when b_n = 1/2, which translates to a_n = (a - 1/2) + 1/2 = a. So, the fixed point is a, as we saw before.

If we start with b_0 = 1/2 (i.e., a_0 = a), then the sequence remains constant. If we start with b_0 ≠ 1/2, then the behavior depends on the value of b_0. For example, if b_0 > 1/2, then b_1 = (b_0)^2 + 1/4. Let's suppose b_0 = 1. Then, b_1 = 1 + 1/4 = 5/4. Then b_2 = (5/4)^2 + 1/4 = 25/16 + 4/16 = 29/16 ≈ 1.8125, and so on, which increases without bound. So, if b_0 > 1/2, the sequence diverges to infinity.

If b_0 = 1/2, it remains constant. If b_0 < 1/2, what happens? Let's take b_0 = 0. Then b_1 = 0 + 1/4 = 1/4. Then b_2 = (1/4)^2 + 1/4 = 1/16 + 4/16 = 5/16 ≈ 0.3125. Then b_3 = (5/16)^2 + 1/4 = 25/256 + 64/256 = 89/256 ≈ 0.3477. Then b_4 ≈ (0.3477)^2 + 0.25 ≈ 0.1209 + 0.25 ≈ 0.3709. It seems to be approaching 1/2 from below. Wait, but when b_n approaches 1/2, what's the behavior near the fixed point?

Since the recurrence is b_{n+1} = b_n² + 1/4. The fixed point is at b = 1/2. Let's check the derivative at the fixed point. The function f(b) = b² + 1/4. The derivative is f’(b) = 2b. At b = 1/2, f’(1/2) = 1. So the magnitude is exactly 1, which means it's a neutral fixed point. This suggests that near b = 1/2, the sequence could converge, diverge, or orbit around the fixed point depending on higher-order terms. But in our example starting at b_0 = 0, the sequence seems to be approaching 1/2. Let's compute a few more terms.

b_0 = 0

b_1 = 0 + 1/4 = 1/4 = 0.25

b_2 = (0.25)^2 + 0.25 = 0.0625 + 0.25 = 0.3125

b_3 = (0.3125)^2 + 0.25 ≈ 0.0977 + 0.25 ≈ 0.3477

b_4 ≈ (0.3477)^2 + 0.25 ≈ 0.1209 + 0.25 ≈ <#sep#> is unlikely, but perhaps as an expression or variable 序列.
